About the Author
 
Aaron L Bratcher has over 20 years of development experience in a
variety of programming languages and industries. After working on
database applications, he spent a small amount of time writing web
applications with Microsoft’s .Net. From there he found his niche in
iOS programming where he has happily remained for over 10 years.
From the day the Swift language was introduced, he strove to make it
his primary development language. He has used it to create iOS apps
for airlines, home automation, the hotel industry, banking, and more.
He has also professionally used SwiftUI in multiple apps. In each
organization he pushed for modernizing and simplifying the code. He
is currently a Senior iOS Developer at JP Morgan Chase & Co.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 12 ---
 
Preface
 
There’s always room for improvement - this statement is a guiding
light to everyone in the software business. It drives the industry
forward as new features are added, bugs are fixed, or new ideas
explored. It encourages hardware manufacturers to make more things
possible than were available before. It sparks the imagination of those
who wish to make their own software and make that glimmer of a
vision come to reality.
 
The aim of this book is to help anyone, from the experienced
professional moving to iOS development to the daring entrepreneur
who has a bold idea on a new app, approach the task of iOS
development with more confidence and an understanding of what it
takes to make an app.
 
Starting with an overview of the deceptively easy-to-approach Swift
language and moving on to the Apple provided way of displaying
items on-screen and interacting with the user with SwiftUI. From there
on, several concepts are introduced to give the reader the necessary
tools to make an improvement to the world of iOS apps.
 

--- Page 13 ---
Chapter 1: Introduction to Swift – Explores the Swift programming
language through the use of a playground, an environment that allows
the quick entry of code with immediate feedback. We learn about
variables, object types, flow control, and more.
 
Chapter 2: Learn SwiftUI Basics for Creating a User Interface – After
reviewing the basics of the Xcode workspace, we learn how to create a
user interface with SwiftUI utilizing several basic generic elements.
We also take advantage of nearly instant previews, eliminating the old
edit, compile, and run cycle for simple changes.
 
Chapter 3: Creating Reusable SwiftUI Views – We learn to small
views that can be reused throughout the app, giving utility and
consistency for both the developer and the end-user.
 
Chapter 4: Design the Household Chores App – In this chapter we
understand the first of the three apps; the household chores app which
is used to assign chores to family members. Review the concept and
create a project that shows the basic interface and interactions along
with the process of localizing text and making an app accessible to all
possible users.
 
Chapter 5: Managing Data and Assets – Building on the previous
chapter, we learn about data models and how to organize assets like
colors and icons.

--- Page 14 ---
 
Chapter 6: Creating Units of Code that can be Shared – This chapter
explores the modularity for the separation of concerns and ease of
giving distinct areas of work to different developers is a common
developmental goal. It also covers how to create a module that houses
specific code that can be integrated into multiple apps.
 
Chapter 7: Saving Data – Leverage Apple’s recently introduced
SwiftData to save data to the local device. Also discover how to create
user defaults that can be set in the iOS Settings app.
 
Chapter 8: Charting Your Progress – Learn about Apple’s Swift Charts
and the variety of ways they can be used. Then update the chores app
to chart family member’s progress on completing their chores.
 
Chapter 9: Create the New York City Schools App – The next app
allows the user to see a list of schools in New York City along with
their associated SAT scores. Create a new project with the appropriate
interface and data structures based on the presented concept.
 
Chapter 10: Testing and Debugging – We learn how to ass unit and UI
tests to ensure the app works as expected and continues to function
properly as changes are made in the future.
 

--- Page 15 ---
Chapter 11: Networking – We learn how to download data from an
internet resource asynchronously and parse it using Apple’s built-in
libraries.
 
Chapter 12: Make it Public – We get familiar with the easy way of
creating test users and publishing the app to them, and the general
public.
 
Chapter 13: Make a Generic App – We learn how to build an app that
can be branded and differentiated across multiple companies. We also
use a modular approach to separate the UI and business logic and
utilize asset catalogs to manage the distinct personality of each
company.
 
OceanofPDF.com

Introduction to Swift

--- Page 40 ---
 
Introduction
 
In 2014, at the Worldwide Developers Conference, Apple introduced
the world to the new Swift programming language with great fanfare.
Since then, the language has been adopted as the premier language for
creating apps not only for Apple products, but for servers and other
platforms too. The future and direction of this language is publicly
presented and debated. See https://swift.org for more information.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 41 ---
 
Structure
 
This chapter contains the following topics:
  
Download Xcode
 
Time to play
 
Variables
 
Custom types
 
Closures
 
Flow control
 
Order of operation
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 42 ---
 
Objectives
 
By the end of this chapter, the reader should know the basic syntax of
the Swift language, some commonly used types, how to create custom
types, and how to manage the flow of how code runs.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 43 ---
 
Download Xcode
 
The App Store is the best place to securely download and install
applications to your computer. After installation, launch the app.
Because Xcode is used to develop apps for all Apple platforms, an
initial set of devices to develop for must be specified. Make sure iOS
is selected and click the Download & Install button, as shown in
Figure 1.1 below:
 

--- Page 44 ---
 
Figure Platform selection dialogue
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 45 ---
 
Time to play
 
From the File menu, select New and then Select the iOS template at the
top and the Blank Playground, then click the Next button on the bottom.
Specify the location to save the playground and, if desired, change the
name. Then click the Create button. In moments a screen that looks like
Figure 1.2 will be shown:
 
 
Figure New Blank Playground
 
The playground is a great way to try some quick code without creating a
full project. The bulk of the display is the editor. To the right, is the Live
In the editor, two lines of code have been provided. For now, we will
ignore the import statement at the top.
 

--- Page 46 ---
Below the import we see: var greeting = "Hello, playground"
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 47 ---
 
Variables
 
A significant goal of programming is to manipulate the environment on
the device or react to a change in the environment. To achieve this, the
app needs to store and manipulate data using values and types. For
instance, the value “Hello, playground” shown above is a string type. The
var keyword declares a mutable variable called When an object is created
and assigned to a variable, it is said to be instantiated.
 
On the bottom-left of the window is the execute button. Click and hold it
to see a pop-up menu. Select Automatically Run and the screen will
change. In the live view, to the right, the same string that was assigned,
“Hello, playground” is displayed with a small square next to it.
 
- this square is the results toggle button. Clicking it will toggle the results
display in the editor.
 
On a blank line at the bottom, enter the following lines of code:
 
greeting = "Swift is easy"
 
var now = Date()
 
let isRunning = true

--- Page 48 ---
 
let maxLength = 256
 
let avgNumberOfChildren = 2.2
 
It may be necessary to disable the automatic running of code while typing
this in. After all the lines of code are entered, make sure to re-enable
Automatically Run or click on the Run button to the left of the last line of
code. When entering a lot of code, the second option may be best. In the
live view, values that are assigned to the variables will be displayed.
Notice how the greeting shows two values? The initial value and then the
new one. If you hover the pointer over any of the values, the line of code
responsible for it will be highlighted in the editor. On the right side of the
value is a preview icon. Click the results toggle or the preview icon. For
now, a repeat of the value is shown. As Swift is explored, this will give
more information.
 
In the code entered, the value type was not specified. Swift will implicitly
assign the type based on the value initially given to it. To see the type,
hold down the option key ( ) and click on the variable name to see a more
formal declaration as shown in Figure Explicitly specifying the type will
be shown later in the chapter.
 

--- Page 49 ---
 
Figure Variable definition popover
 
The keyword let was used instead of var for three of the lines above. What
does this mean? On a blank line, at the bottom of the variable
declarations, try adding a line to change the value of isRunning to I am
now
 
What happens? Figure 1.4 illustrates the error:
 
 
Figure (Immutable variable error)
 
A variable defined using the let syntax makes it immutable, or constant,
after a value has been assigned. When a variable’s value does not need to
change, use let. To allow isRunning’s value to change, go to the
declaration and change it to
 
What happens? Figure 1.5 shows an additional error:

--- Page 50 ---
 
 
Figure (Variable type error)
 
Some languages allow the value type of a variable to be changed after it
has been set. However, Swift is a strongly typed language, which means
the value type cannot change. The variable isRunning is a Bool type so it
can only hold the values of true or false. Remove the errant line of code.
 
When the first error was displayed, a debug area was shown to give
additional information. To show or hide this area, click the Debug area
toggle button below the Live View panel.
 
The names used for the variables are arbitrary. Swift convention says
variable names start with a lowercase letter and an uppercase for
concatenated words in the variable name. This is called camel case. In
Swift, variable names can be any combination of letters, numbers, and
even emojis. Names have these primary restrictions: They cannot begin
with a number and must not contain mathematical symbols or white space
characters, that is, spaces, tabs, newlines, and so on. Try it now. Change
the names of the variables and introduce numbers in the middle or at the
end of the names or try an emoji character. What works and what does
not?
 

--- Page 51 ---
So far, we have been assigning values to variables at the time of
declaration. While this may work in limited cases, the value is usually not
known until a later time. On a blank link, enter these lines of code and run
by either setting the playground to run automatically or clicking the Run
button to the left of the last line of code:
 
let maxToppings: Int
 
var useBox: Bool
 
maxToppings = 10
 
useBox = false
 
When a value is not immediately assigned to the variable, the type must
be specified at the time of declaration. But wait, there is more.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 52 ---
 
Collections
 
When deciding on the name of a variable, it is important to describe
the type of value and what the value represents. For instance, if the
name maxLength were changed to dogNames how would your
expectations of the value change?
 
The first expectation is for the variable to hold String values. The
second is to hold multiple values because of the plurality in the name.
When a variable holds multiple values, it is called a collection. The
three principal collection types in Swift are array, dictionary and set.
Type in the following line of code on a blank line and run:
 
var dogNames = ["Daisy", "Caesar", "Luna"]
 
var dogAges = [2, 5,  7]
 
See how the Live View shows array of 3 elements?
 
Now, click on the results toggle to see the values. Try different values
inside the square brackets. Notice how there is always a comma
between each value and how the types must match. Use Boolean,
string, integer, or floating-point numbers. Some languages would call

--- Page 53 ---
these primitive types, however in Swift they are treated just like any
other object type we will learn about later.
 
There are different ways of accessing the data in an array. The easiest
is to use the ordinal position. On the line after the dogAges variable,
type in: let firstDog = Notice that array index starts at zero.
 
Like any other variable, a collection can have values assigned after the
declaration. Remove the values between the square brackets and watch
what happens. An error is displayed. Swift does not know what type of
values the variable will store. It needs to be specified either by the
developer or by the compiler. Here, the compiler does not know what
type to assign so you need to.
 
Change the line of code to look like this:
 
var dogNames: [String] = []
 
Now we have an empty array for strings. Let us explore different ways
of assigning and removing values to this variable. Type in these lines
of code and run:
 
dogNames = ["Daisy", "Caesar", "Luna"]
 
dogNames.append("Ralph")

--- Page 54 ---
 
dogNames.insert("Sparky", at: 0)
 
dogNames.remove(at: 1)
 
let sortedNames = dogNames.sorted()
 
Before looking at the results, what do you think each line of code
does? The first and last lines are probably obvious, but what about the
others? After giving it some thought, click on the results toggle button
next to each entry in the Live View. Are the results what you expected?
 
Something that may be surprising is the value shown for the remove
statement because it shows the value affected by the statement instead
of the changed variable. The returned value is being ignored. It can be
captured by changing the line to something like this: let removedDog =
dogNames.remove(at: may have been surprised that Daisy was
removed and not Remember, arrays start at index zero.
 
In the code above for inserting, change the 0 to 5 and see what
happens. We will explore debugging in a later chapter.
 
The next collection to discuss is the dictionary. It uses a unique
identifier, or key, and associates it with a value. On a blank line, enter
the following lines of code and run:

--- Page 55 ---
 
let stations = [2: "Shell", 16: "BP", 32: "Chevron"]
 
print(stations[2])
 
Like arrays, each entry is separated by a comma. Each entry consists
of the key, a colon, then the value. The type for all provided values
must match. If you are wondering why the print statement said then
you are in for a treat. That will be discussed soon. To get a preview, try
changing the 2 to
 
The type used for this dictionary’s key is however AnyHashable type
can be used. In fact, when declaring the dictionary variable,
AnyHashable can be specified as the type. Change the let statement to
the following and run:
 
var stations: [AnyHashable: String]
 
stations = [2:"Shell", "16":"BP", 32:"Chevron"]
 
The declaration specifies the key type and the value type. Later, we
will look at using other types for the values. Notice how the keys used
on the second line are now Int and Print the BP value by changing the
key used in the print statement to In Swift, Int and String are hashable

--- Page 56 ---
types. As it is possible to make custom types they can also serve as
keys, if desired. Most often Int and String are used as the key type.
 
Like arrays, dictionaries are not always populated at declaration and
instantiation. Enter the following lines of code and run:
 
1. stations[55] = "Phillips 66"
 
2. stations[55] = "PetroChina"
 
3. stations.removeValue(forKey: 2)
 
4. let stationCount = stations.count
 
5. print(stations.keys)
 
6. print(stations.values)
 
7. stations = [:]
 
There is a lot happening here, so let us dissect it one line at a time. The
first line assigns the value Phillips 66 to the dictionary with the key
The second line assigns the value PetroChina to the dictionary with the
key However, we must wonder, how is that that possible? Keys are
supposed to be unique.

--- Page 57 ---
 
The second line replaces the value associated with the key This is
confirmed with the last print statement. Line three completely removes
the key, and the value associated with it.
 
The fourth line assigns the number of dictionary entries to the variable
All collections have the count property. In Swift, properties are
variables associated with an object. Other helpful properties for
collections are: and
 
The print statements on lines five and six above print out the keys and
values properties respectively. Note that they are arrays. The keys and
values printed may or may not be in the order entered because, unlike
an array, a dictionary is an unordered collection. The last statement
instantiates an empty dictionary and assigns it to the variable.
 
The final collection type we will cover is the With the dictionary, any
object type that is hashable can be used as the key that is associated
with some data. What if we do not have any associated data? A
situation where the key is the data, and we need a unique collection of
these objects. This is the perfect time to use a Let us try it.
 
Type in the following lines and run:
 
var partyGuests: Set = []

--- Page 58 ---
 
partyGuests.insert("Aaron L Bratcher")
 
partyGuests.insert("Anne McCaffrey")
 
partyGuests.insert("Douglas Adams")
 
partyGuests.insert("Richard Castle")
 
print(partyGuests)
 
partyGuests.remove("Douglas Adams")
 
print(partyGuests)
 
let insertResults = partyGuests.insert("Richard Castle")
 
print(insertResults)
 
To limit the output in the Debug area, remove all code entered before
or create a new playground. Again, we see something new in the
declaration and initialization of the variable. A Set can hold any
hashable type. Like the dictionary, the type must be specified. The type
to be stored, in this case a is specified in the angle brackets. Set utilizes

--- Page 59 ---
generics, a feature covered later in this book. The square brackets
initialize the variable with an empty set.
 
As a Set is not ordered, the concept of appending is not logical.
Instead, an object is inserted into the set and conversely, a known
object can be removed. When an object is inserted, the response can be
ignored, as was shown the first several times or captured. One could
initially think the returned value of an insert would be a Bool type to
indicate the success of the insert, however upon inspection you will
find something new. Option-click on the variable and you will see this
definition:
 
let insertResults: (inserted: Bool, memberAfterInsert: String)
 
The parenthesis around two element definitions is a Tuple. Tuples can
have one or multiple elements with, or without, a name. If a name is
included, each element is like a variable definition. Here are two
examples:
 
Example 1:
 
    let someData: (String, String, String)
 
    someData = (“first element”, “second object”, “third”)
 

--- Page 60 ---
Example 2:
 
  var moreData: (name: String, city: String)
 
  moreData = ("Thomas", "New York City")
 
  moreData = (name: "Roger", city: "Los Angeles")
 
It can be easy to forget what each value represents without a name, so
names are recommended. However, when a name is not available, the
ordinal index can be used. Like arrays, the index of tuple elements is
zero based.
  
print(someData.1)
 
print(moreData.0)
 
print(moreData.city)
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 61 ---
 
Optionals
 
An optional starts with a question. Does a value exist? Remember
when stations [2] was printed earlier? The output was
Optional("Shell")\n and when stations [5] was printed, the output was
Why? Because when an attempt to retrieve a value from a dictionary is
performed, the key or the value associated with it may not exist. It is
only appropriate that a question mark is used to define when a variable
or property is optional. Here is a declaration of a variable/property that
may or may not have a String value:
 
let crust: String?
 
An attempt to print the value of crust, will generate an error. An
immutable optional needs to be initialized. In this case either with a
string or nil. Nil indicates no value exists. If the let was to be changed
to a then it would compile and run properly as it starts with nil
assigned.
 
To properly retrieve the value, the optional must be unwrapped or
coalesced to a non-optional value. Here are a few accepted ways of
doing this:
 
let crust: String?

--- Page 62 ---
 
crust = "thin"
 
if let crust {
 
  print(crust)
 
}
 
if var crust {
 
  crust = "altered crust"
 
  print(crust)
 
}
 
let crustType = crust ?? ""
 
The first option is to use the if let syntax. This checks if a value exists
and, if one does, assigns it to a variable of the same name. This new
variable is limited to the scope of the if statement, that is, between the
curly braces. The variable can now be used safely. Outside of the
scope, the original optional variable still exists.

--- Page 63 ---
 
The second option uses the if var syntax. It is like the first option, but
it gives a mutable variable that can be used within the scope of the if
statement. Also, while the value of the new variable may be changed
within the scope, it does not affect the original variable or property.
 
The last option shown here is to coalesce. When the original variable
has no value, the option after the coalesce operator is used instead.
Multiple coalesce operators can be used in the same expression to
chain multiple optionals together. Add a print statement to see what the
value of crustType is. There is also the use of a guard statement, but it
will be shown in a later segment because of how it is used.
 
Something not shown, because it is frowned on, however sometimes it
is needed, is the force-unwrap operator. This will attempt to unwrap
the variable and assign the value. However, if it fails, the app will
crash. Remove the assignment of thin to the variable and then run the
statement below and an error will appear.
 
let crustType = crust!
 
A lot has been covered for how a variable is defined and used with
some built-in types. We have also looked at collections and optionals.
These basics are used in all aspects of Swift development and needs to

--- Page 64 ---
be completely understood. Try to do the following on your own
(answers are at the end of the chapter):
  
Create a variable that holds an array of optional strings.
 
Create a variable that holds a dictionary with an integer key and an
optional tuple of two strings.

--- Page 66 ---
 
Structs
 
A struct is a way to organize naturally related data elements together
and, optionally, provide ways of manipulating them. They are very
efficient for this task. For instance, a pizza representation may have a
struct like this:
 
struct Pizza {
 
  let crust: String?
 
  Let sauce: String
 
  let toppings: [String]
 
}
 
The key word struct specifies what kind of custom type we are
creating. Notice the name, is capitalized. When creating a new type,
Swift convention is to capitalize the name of the type. When creating
an instance object of the type, the variable or property starts with a
lowercase letter.
 

--- Page 67 ---
Some properties were created to describe a typical pizza. What kind of
crust? Thin, hand-tossed, cheese filled, or, maybe, none. Some sauce
and toppings.
 
Like a string or dictionary, Pizza is now a type that can be created and
assigned to a variable or property. The struct elements are properties
that must be initialized with values. Swift helps us out by
automatically creating an initializer for the struct so we can simply do
this to instantiate the object:
 
let pizza = Pizza(crust: nil, sauce: "tomato", toppings: ["onion", "green
pepper", "mushroom", "black olive"])
 
print(pizza.toppings)
 
This creates a Pizza object and assigns it to our variable, This could be
a pizza bowl, where no crust exists. Like many programming
languages, Swift is case-sensitive. It is very common to have a variable
name that closely matches the type name.
 
There are times a coded initializer is needed. An initializer provides a
point where parameters may be passed to make a valid object. It
consists of the keyword, followed by an open parenthesis, maybe some
parameters, then the closing parenthesis.
 

--- Page 68 ---
The first case, shown below, is a one-to-one mapping of the init
parameters to the properties that need a value. Swift implicitly
provides this case for simple structs. The parameters provided to the
init can also have default values assigned. Add the init code so the
struct looks like this:
 
struct Pizza {
 
  let crust: String?
 
  let sauce: String
 
  let toppings: [String]
 
  init(crust: String? = nil, sauce: String = "tomato",
 
toppings: [String]) {
 
      self.crust = crust
 
      self.sauce = sauce
 
      self.toppings = toppings
 

--- Page 69 ---
  }
 
}
 
What is the first thing you notice? That the parameter declarations look
remarkably like the variable work done earlier. The first parameter, is
an optional string. A default value of nil is given. This would be
helpful for pizza bowls where a crust does not exist. The default value
for sauce is tomato and toppings does not have a default value so it
must be specified when creating the object. Using this initializer, the
creation of the pizza bowl can now look like this:
 
let pizza = Pizza(toppings: ["onion", "green pepper", "mushroom",
 
"black olive"])
 
The default values are for convenience but do not keep the developer
from using the parameters. Any or all the parameters may be used for
the instantiation of the
 
let pizza = Pizza(crust: "thin", sauce: "tomato",
 
toppings: ["onion", "green pepper", "mushroom", "black olive"])
 

--- Page 70 ---
The second thing to be noticed, may be the use of self. This is a
reference to the one specific object and the items inside it. Remember
when the dictionary keys were printed? The code referenced the keys
property of the single dictionary object. The initializer is assigning
values to the properties of self. When there is a need to reference
something within the object itself, self is used when there is ambiguity.
Otherwise, it is implied, (there is another use for it that will be looked
at later in the book). For instance, what if the first parameter in init had
been called base instead of crust? If that were done, then base could
have been assigned to the crust property without use of the self
designation.
 
Immutable properties on this struct were used. We can also use
mutable properties that have a default value, so they are changeable
from outside the struct. When all the properties have a default value, a
second init without parameters can be created. Adjust your code like
so:
 
struct Pizza {
 
  var crust: String?
 
  var sauce: String = "tomato"
 
  var toppings: [String] = []
 

--- Page 71 ---
  init(crust: String? = nil, sauce: String = "tomato",
 
toppings: [String]) {
 
      self.crust = crust
 
      self.sauce = sauce
 
      self.toppings = toppings
 
  }
 
  init() { }
 
}
 
let pizza = Pizza()
 
pizza.toppings = ["onion", "green pepper", "mushroom", "black olive"]
 
print(pizza.sauce)
 

--- Page 72 ---
When using a mutable, optional property, the default value is nil, so it
does not need to be assigned. The second init function has an empty
body. It is provided so an instance of Pizza can be created without
using any parameters. Now a pizza can be instantiated using the init
with parameters or the one without. Swift will determine,
automatically, which one to use at the point of instantiation. When you
make these changes and attempt to run, you will get an error. Do you
know why? The pizza variable is an immutable struct. Changes cannot
be made to it. Change the let to a var on line sixteen and the problem
will be fixed. There is so much more to structs that we will look at
later. For now, it is time to fix another problem.
OceanofPDF.com

Enums
 
The Pizza struct uses String properties. However, each one has a
limited number of options. When the is a known, fixed, number of
options, it is best to enumerate them. Clear your playground of all
code, except the import at the top, and add this:
 
enum Crust {
 
  case thin
 
  case handTossed
 
  case deepDish
 
  case none
 
}
 
enum Sauce {
 
  case tomato, white, arrabbiata, none

--- Page 74 ---
 
}
 
let crust = Crust.handTossed
 
let sauce: Sauce
 
sauce = .tomato
 
Like a struct, you start with your keyword then the name of your
custom type. Again, the name of the type is capitalized. See how each
name assigned is singular, not plural. When creating a variable, you
can use implicit type allocation if you give a value immediately or
assign the type and give a value later. Notice how the full enum name
Sauce.tomato is not required when assigning the value to the sauce
variable? Swift is contextually aware of the Sauce type so only the
specific enumeration is necessary.
 
It was mentioned above that a pizza may not have a crust. While an
optional enum is possible, it is best to have an enumeration when
possible. Below the Crust and Sauce enums, enter these lines of code
and run:
 
enum Size: Int {

--- Page 75 ---
 
  case six = 6
 
  case twelve = 12
 
  case eighteen = 18
 
}
 
let size = Size.twelve
 
print(size.rawValue)
 
let size2 = Size(rawValue: 16)
 
print(size2?.rawValue ?? "unknown")
 
if size == .six {
 
  print("personal size")
 
}
 

--- Page 76 ---
If you know another language, this may be more familiar to you. The
:Int indicates an enumeration with an integer raw type. If values are
not assigned, as done here, they are automatically assigned
sequentially starting with zero. Raw values are useful when retrieving
data from a server or using input from the user, but what makes Swift
especially great is that it is not limited to integers. Strings are another
popular raw data item for enumerations. In fact, with some extra work,
any object can be the raw type.
 
The rawValue property is available on a variable or property that is a
raw type Conversely, an instance of the enum can be created from a
raw value. However, because there is no guarantee that it will match,
the result is an optional as shown on lines ten and eleven.
 
See how there is a question mark when printing size2? It is an
optional, the rawValue property may not be accessible because size2
has no value to get the raw value from. The question mark is a
reminder that you are working with an optional variable. To guarantee
a value, we have coalesced to a non-optional value. The last part tests
if the size is equal to In Swift, the == operator tests for equality. Note
that the rawValue was not tested. Do you think the print statement will
execute?
 
Another great feature of enums on Swift is the ability to associated
data with the enumeration. Add this but do not force the playground to
run yet:

--- Page 77 ---
 
enum Topping {
 
  case sausage(isSpicy: Bool, grade: String)
 
  case cheese, onion, mushroom, greenPepper
 
}
 
The sausage enumeration has a tuple next to it. As discussed before, a
tuple can have one or more values with or without a name given to
each value. The name makes the code easier to read. When
programming, remember that others may need to read your code, so it
must be as concise and as easy-to-understand as possible. Enter the
following code below the enum declaration and run to see this
variation of the enum in action:
 
let topping: Topping = .sausage(isSpicy: true, grade: "hot")
 
switch topping {
 
case .cheese:
 
print("add cheese")

--- Page 78 ---
 
case .sausage(let spicy, _):
 
  print("sausage is spicy: \(spicy)")
 
  print("how spicy isn’t unspecified")
 
case .greenPepper:
 
  fallthrough
 
case .onion, .mushroom:
 
  print("veggies!")
 
default:
 
  break
 
}
 
The assignment on the first line is straightforward with the associated
values. Below that is a switch statement. A switch statement allows the

--- Page 79 ---
developer to test an object against all possible values. After the switch
keyword, specify the object to test. Each test has the word followed by
what the object is tested against, then ends with a colon. One or more
statements to be executed are in the subsequent lines.
 
Each case statement must have an executable statement below it. If
there is nothing to do, simply add the break command. If a case is to
execute the same commands as the subsequent one, you can use the
fallthrough command as shown with Also, a test against multiple items
can be part of the same case statement.
 
When an enum with associated values is being used, there must be a
way of extracting those values. Note the use of a tuple in the .sausage
case. The names here do not need to match the original definition
because they are only used in the scope of the case statement. If one of
the elements is not being used, an underline can be used to indicate no
use. If none of the elements are to be used the tuple portion does not
need to be used at all.
 
There is something new on line eight above. It is called string
interpolation. It allows the developer to put an expression in the
middle of a string. This is an easy way of putting data from variables
or properties into a string. There are some caveats that we will explore
later in the book.
 

--- Page 80 ---
Switch statements are very flexible and can be used in a variety of
ways. For instance, a switch on a string could be used to test against
multiple values, because a switch must be exhaustive, a default
statement would be required. Or, for numbers, a range of values can be
tested as shown here:
 
let waistSize = 32
 
switch waistSize {
 
case 0..<20:
 
  print("tiny")
 
case 20..<40:
 
  print("medium")
 
case 40...60:
 
  print("large")
 
default:
 
  print("not stocked")

--- Page 81 ---
 
}
 
Here, an integer value is assigned to waistSize and tested against some
ranges. The ..< operator is a non-inclusive range. That is to say, the
first case starts at 0 and includes every integer up to, but not including,
The same for the next case statement. It starts at 20 and includes every
integer up to, but not including The … operator is an inclusive range.
It starts at 40 and includes all integers up to and including A default
was provided because a switch must include all the possibilities. With
the topping example mentioned earlier, a warning would be shown
because the switch is already exhaustive without the use of the default
option.
 
Here are a couple more ways of extracting the associated value from
an enum assignment:
 
let topping2: Topping = .sausage(isSpicy: true, grade: "hot")
 
if case .sausage = topping2 {
 
  print("some sort of sausage")
 
}

--- Page 82 ---
 
if case let .sausage(isSpicy, grade) = topping2 {
 
  print("is spicy: \(isSpicy) grade: \(grade)")
 
}
 
Earlier a variable was compared to a specific enumeration,
if size == .six . However, when an enum case has associated values,
this is not possible. Instead, we must use either a switch statement, as
shown with the topping switch earlier, or if case statements as shown
here. The syntax for if case can be confusing, however it may help to
remember that a new variable, is being created and assigned
conditionally based on the enumeration.
 
With their incredible flexibility and utility, enums make Swift a great
language to work with. In fact, you may find yourself making an enum
of enums or instead of using case statements, use static values for
organizational purposes like this:
 
enum Preference {
 
  enum Color {
 

--- Page 83 ---
      static let blue = "Blue"
 
      static let green = "green"
 
      static let red = "red"
 
  }

      static let small = 20
 
      static let medium = 35
 
      static let large = 60
 
  }

      static let thin = 0.1
 
      static let normal = 0.25
 

--- Page 84 ---
      static let thick = 0.5
 
  }
 
}

Classes
 
Clear the code entered into the playground or create a new one.
Classes are the foundation of object-oriented programming. They are
like structs with some key differences. Class objects are referenced
objects versus value, and they can be subclassed, inheriting the
properties and methods of the parent class while gaining new ones
specific to them.
 
Properties have been discussed, but not methods or functions. A
function is a piece of code that can accept data, performs some work,
and may return a result. A method is a function within the context of an
object. The method has access to the object’s properties and other
methods and can change the state of the object. There are many times a
method accepts no parameters, nor returns a result because its goal is
to change the state of the object or, possibly, the environment of where
the code is being run. In Swift, even structs and enums can have
methods.
 
Here is an example of a class with a subclass. Enter this code, without
the line numbers, and run:
  
class Map {
 

--- Page 86 ---
  func canMove(_ player: Player, to position: CGPoint) -> Bool {
 
      return true
 
  }
 
}
 
class Player {
 
  var numberOfLives: Int
 
  var positionOnMap: CGPoint
 
  init() {
 
      numberOfLives = 3
 
      positionOnMap = .zero
 
  }
 
  init(numberOfLives: Int, positionOnMap: CGPoint) {

--- Page 87 ---
 
      self.numberOfLives = numberOfLives
 
      self.positionOnMap = positionOnMap
 
  }
 
  func moveTo(_ position: CGPoint) -> Bool {
 
      positionOnMap = position
 
      return true
 
  }
 
}
 
class Soldier: Player {
 
  private var numberOfMoves: Int = 0
 
  override func moveTo(_ position: CGPoint) -> Bool {
 

--- Page 88 ---
      if numberOfMoves < 5 {
 
        positionOnMap = position
 
        numberOfMoves += 1
 
        return true
 
      } else {
 
        return false
 
      }
 
  }
 
}
 
let map = Map()
 
let player1 = Player()
 
let player2: Player = Soldier(numberOfLives: 5, positionOnMap:
CGPoint(x: 20, y: 5))

  didMove = player1.moveTo(position)
 
}
 
print("player 1 did move: \(didMove)")

The same pattern as the other custom types is used. Like a struct, start
with the keyword, class, then the name of the custom type. The name
of the class is capitalized. The Map class does not have any properties
so it does not need an However, even without properties, there may be
cases where an initializer is needed. This will be explained further
when libraries are studied. It has one method that takes two parameters
and returns a Bool. For simplicity, it just returns a Interestingly, in
Swift, when a function has only one line of code, you can omit the

Try it. Remove the word return on line three and then run all the code.
Again, this only works when there is one line of code that returns a

The CGPoint is a built-in struct that is used to identify the coordinates
on a 2D plane. This book will cover a limited set of items like this.
Each OS in Apple’s array of devices has a rich supply of libraries and

--- Page 91 ---
The function type on line two, the unique name, parameters, and return
type, has some new items to discuss. An underscore in the first
parameter. This means that there is no name used at the call-point. The
word after the underscore is name of the variable to be used inside the
scope of the function. The second parameter has two names. The first
is to be used at the call-point and, again, the second is the name of the
variable to be used inside the function. Lines forty-seven and fifty-five
above show how we call this function. In the English language, the
call-point reads like a sentence.
 
The player class looks a lot like a struct. There are two One provides
some default values for the properties, while the other needs them to
be passed in as the object is instantiated. Lines forty-one and forty-two
show this in action.
 
The Soldier class is where we see object-oriented programming in
action. The colon followed by Player indicates that the Soldier class is
a subclass of It has the same properties and methods as the parent
class, but has the option of having additional properties, publicly
available or private to use only within the class, or even overriding the
parent’s items.
 
After instantiating two players, the code checks if the map will allow
the player to move to the proposed position, and if possible, tells the
object to move to that position. Player2 is typed as Player on line
forty-three but assigned a Soldier class object. This is because the

--- Page 92 ---
Soldier class is a type of Any place where a Player is wanted, an
instance of the Soldier class can be used. Like the call to the map
function.
 
Line forty-seven assigns the return value of the method call to the
didMove variable. The method definition is simple, assign the new
position to the property and return true. Line fifty-five is where we see
object-oriented programming at its best. As player2 is the Player type.
You can test this by option-clicking on the player2 variable, but
because it was assigned a Soldier class object, the code inside Soldier
will run. This is called polymorphism.
 
With polymorphism, the subclass code is run even when the variable
type is of the superclass. How would you verify the code inside the
Soldier class is run? Of course, the easy way would be to simply put a
print statement inside the method. But how would you get the
didMove variable to be false? Each time the moveTo method is called,
a private property is incremented. If that value is equal to or more than
five, then a false is returned. Knowing this, we can repeat the code on
line fifty-six several more times and run. The output should now show
a false for player2 being moved.
 
What if the developer needs to access a specific property or method of
the soldier object? An attempt to reassign the variable as the desired
class could be tried, as shown here:
 
if let soldier = player2 as? Soldier {

--- Page 93 ---
 
  print("Yes, sir!")
 
}
 
Here, the code checks if player2 is the soldier type and if so, create a
scope-specific variable called soldier. The soldier variable is only valid
in the scope between the curly braces. The illustration here is not good
programming, however, because a variable was created and never
used. A better way, in this case, would be to test the type directly:
 
if player2 is Soldier {
 
  print("I'm a soldier")
 
}
 
One last thing about the Soldier class. It has a private declaration. This
is called access control. By default, properties and methods are
internal, meaning they are accessible by everything in the same
module, a concept covered later in the book. Private is only accessible
from within the object itself, and public is everywhere the object is
instantiable.
 

--- Page 94 ---
Classes are reference types. This means that any variable for a class
object holds a reference to the object. Reassigning to another variable
will point to the same object in memory. Here is an easy way to see
this in action. At the bottom of your playground, keeping all the
existing code, type and run this:
 
let player3 = player2
 
player3.numberOfLives = 5
 
print("player3 lives: \(player3.numberOfLives)")
 
print("player2 lives: \(player2.numberOfLives)")
 
The printout will show both players having the same number of lives.
This is the reference semantic of classes. When player2 is assigned the
Soldier object, it occupies space in memory and when player2 is no
longer in scope, the object referenced is removed from memory.
However, when the variable is passed to a method, or assigned to
another variable, then additional references to that object exist. The
object cannot be removed from memory until all the references are
gone. This technique of memory management is called reference
counting.
 

--- Page 95 ---
One last comment on reference types. The player3 was declared with a
let statement and the property was still updated. This is because the
actual value held by player3 is a reference to the object, not the object
itself. This reference will not change unless a new object, of the same
type, is assigned to it. Swift knows the object is by reference and
dereferences it for us. This is a convenience of having a modern
language.
 
Structs, arrays, and dictionaries are value types. They occupy memory,
usually less than reference types because they do not need the
management data used behind the scenes as classes have, and when the
variable goes out of scope the memory is released. If one of these is
assigned to another variable, then it is copied so any changes made
through the second variable are specific to that variable. Here is a good
example:
 
struct Gamer {
 
  var numberOfLives = 0
 
}
 
var gamer1 = Gamer()
 
gamer1.numberOfLives = 3

--- Page 96 ---
 
var gamer2 = gamer1
 
gamer2.numberOfLives = 10
 
print("gamer1 lives: \(gamer1.numberOfLives)")
 
print("gamer2 lives: \(gamer2.numberOfLives)")
 
The output varies from gamer1 to When should a struct or a class be
used? If data is being downloaded to be presented to the user, that is an
ideal time to use a If an object that may be altered by different points
of the program is needed, that is the perfect case for using a
Experiment and see what works best for code readability and
simplicity.
 
Note: Technically, a struct object is not copied until the data needs to
change. This is called copy on write. Doing this allows the code to run
faster at times when an actual copy is not necessary.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 97 ---
 
Protocols
 
With subclassing available, it is fun to look for ways of utilizing it.
Make a superclass with all the basic properties and methods then make
subclasses for the variants. However, sometimes, situations arise
where similar functionality exists for classes that do not fit together.
When this happens, more properties and methods are added to the
superclass that do not belong in an effort of supporting the disparate
subclasses.
 
A protocol provides a way of defining a collection of properties and/or
methods that a class, struct, enum, and several other object types can
implement. This decouples the complex logic each type would require
implementing the functionality from a single superclass while keeping
the strong type system in-place. Also, protocols allow for extensions so
default implementations can be provided in the definition itself. Add
the following code in the same playground or a new one:
 
UsesFuel {
 
  var fuelType: String { get }
 
  var percentRemaining: Double { get set }

--- Page 98 ---
 
}
 
extension UsesFuel {
 
  mutating func consumeFuel(percent: Double) {
 
      percentRemaining -= percent
 
  }
 
}
 
protocol Flies {
 
  var prefilghtComplete: Bool { get }
 
  func performPreflight()
 
}
 
In the body of the protocol, properties and/or methods are declared that
must be defined in whatever object conforms to this protocol. For

--- Page 99 ---
properties there are two options: Readable { get } or readable /
writeable { get set
 
As the code was entered, you may have noticed line six declares an
extension to the UsesFuel protocol and the function is not declared
anywhere else. The extension deserves some explanation. Any object
type can have an extension where calculated properties or methods can
be added. An extension can even be created for the built-in types. The
caveat of an extension is that it cannot declare properties that store
data. However, computed properties can be declared. These properties
use existing data or methods to compute and return a result. Extensions
are also a great way of breaking up the definition of a custom type. For
instance, you could declare your enum or then use an extension to
conform to a protocol, or to simply add convenience properties or
methods.
 
Below the Flies protocol, add this code and run:
 
class Jet: UsesFuel, Flies {
 
  var prefilghtComplete: Bool = false
 
  var fuelType: String {
 
      "Jet Fuel"

--- Page 100 ---
 
  }
 
  var percentRemaining: Double = 1
 
  func performPreflight() {
 
      prefilghtComplete = true
 
  }
 
}
 
let jet = Jet()
 
jet.performPreflight()
 
print(jet.prefilghtComplete)
 
The method in the UsesFuel protocol extension has the word mutating
in front of it. This is so a struct can also conform to this protocol, as
struct methods must declare they are mutating the values of the object.
With this declaration, multiple kinds of objects can use this protocol.
Try removing the word mutating and see what happens.

--- Page 101 ---
 
To conform to a protocol, the developer places a colon, then the name
of the protocol after the name. Subclasses must add a comma after the
superclass name, then the name of the protocol or protocols. This is
shown with the struct and class declarations above. If you try to
remove the properties defined in these object types, an error will be
displayed.
 
Protocols are types, they can be used wherever a type is declared. Here
is a simple example. Add this to the bottom of your playground and
run:
 
class Hangar {
 
  func repairPlane(plane: Flies) {
 
      print("plane repaired")
 
  }
 
}
 
let hangar = Hangar()
 

--- Page 102 ---
hangar.repairPlane(plane: jet)
 
jet) jet) jet) jet) jet) jet) jet) jet) jet) jet) jet) jet)
  
Object-oriented programming is about organizing data and structures.
The methods can be used to change the state of the objects, return
information, or both. It is a system of programming that has been
around for a long time and there is still a place for it in today’s code. It
is a tool in the box along with structs, protocols, actors, and so on.
Protocols allow a way to define a type that is focused and can be
applied across a broad set of various kinds of objects that may have no
relationship to each other avoiding the overly complex superclass.
And, because your custom type can conform to multiple protocols,
your types can be given additional complexity, as needed.
 
Another style of programming is called functional programming. The
basic premise is that you do not change the state. Everything needed
for the function to work is passed in and, for a given state of
parameters, the output is always the same. Testing is a lot easier with
this form of programming and it can be applied to other areas of Swift.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 103 ---
 
Closures
 
Functions are used everywhere. From the built-in frameworks to
libraries you download to your own code, functions are the backbone
of code execution. It should be no surprise that Swift can treat
functions as first-class objects. They can be passed as parameters to
other functions, assigned to variables, or even returned from functions.
 
Below is an example of how this can be beneficial and how Swift
makes it easier to code. Erase your playground or create a new one and
add the following code after the import:
 
typealias Weight = Double
 
typealias FermentProcess = () -> Void
 
struct PizzaDough {
 
let grams: Weight
 
  init(grams: Weight) {
 

--- Page 104 ---
      self.grams = grams
 
  }
 
  func ferment(process: FermentProcess) {
 
      process()
 
  }
 
}
 
Again, the code here starts with something new. The keyword typealias
allows us to declare a new type as a shortcut to something easily
expressed, because it is a type, it is capitalized. Code after the equal
sign is what the compiler replaces the alias with. This is a great way to
take something that may easily lose its meaning when read in code and
give a context. Here, the Double type can be expressed as
 
The next alias is for a function. The empty parenthesis, arrow, and
Void are the definition of a function that takes no parameters and does
not return anything. This is used on line eleven of the code entered.
Now enter this code and run:
 

--- Page 105 ---
let dough = PizzaDough(grams: 500)
 
let fermentProcess: FermentProcess = {
 
  print("saved function to ferment pizza dough")
 
}
 
dough.ferment(process: fermentProcess)
 
dough.ferment(process: {
 
  print("fermenting dough with weight of \(dough.grams) grams")
 
})
 
dough.ferment {
 
  print("fermenting more dough with weight of \(dough.grams) grams")
 
}
 

--- Page 106 ---
Like we have done with other types, PizzaDough is instantiated and
assigned to the dough variable. Below that, on lines three through five,
a function is created and saved to the fermentProcess variable. Stop
and scrutinize this. Understand it. FermentProcess is a stand-alone
function type that takes no parameters and does not return anything. It
just has a body, the content between the curly braces. In Swift, having
a function outside of an object like this is called a closure. The
advantage is that a closure can have access to local properties of the
parent context.
 
On line seven, the variable is passed to another function. The ferment
function expects only one parameter that is also a function. This is a
powerful feature that can be utilized in many ways. However, to make
it easier, a variable does not need to be created first, the closure can be
passed in-line as done on lines nine through eleven. The open brace
indicates the start of the function body, and a closing brace ends it.
This is followed by the closing parenthesis for the parameter list.
 
This can be simplified even more. In Swift, when the last parameter of
a function call is a closure, the parameter can be left out of the
initialization list and expressed outside of the parameter list. This is
called a trailing closure. If there are no other parameters required, the
parenthesis can also be dropped. This is illustrated on lines thirteen
through fifteen.
 

--- Page 107 ---
Closures can also accept their own parameters. Unfortunately, they
cannot be labeled at the point of use. Still, a name can be given in the
definition to make the code more easily read. Enter this:
 
typealias BakeTechnique = (_ pizzaSize: Weight) -> Void
 
struct PizzaOven {
 
  let bake: BakeTechnique
 
  init(bake: @escaping BakeTechnique) {
 
      self.bake = bake
 
  }
 
}
 
The BakeTechnique type is an alias for a function that takes one
parameter of type and does not return anything. The closure is saved as
a local property to the struct. Because this function can be called at any
time in the future, the parameter is marked as escaping.
 
With code completion, Xcode is helpful when typing in code as
illustrated in Figure 1.6 below. As you start to enter a type, Xcode will

--- Page 108 ---
give a suggestion with possible parameters. Even with the misspelling,
it showed the custom type. After pressing the type and parameter list is
filled in with the type highlighted. Pressing Enter again will resolve
BakeTechnnique to a trailing closure with the variable to be used in the
body.
 
 
Figure Auto completion
 
Continue until the entered code looks like the following and run:
  
let oven = PizzaOven { pizzaSize in
 
  print("baking pizza")
 
}
 
oven.bake(400)
  

--- Page 109 ---
Line five illustrates the closure taking the value, without a label. How
could the function type be altered to return a Bool? On your own, alter
the BakeTechnique function type to return number of minutes required
to bake and use it in the PizzaOven struct. A possible answer is at the
end of the chapter.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 110 ---
 
Flow control
 
Until now, only the if and switch statements have been used to control
what code is executed. Clearly, more options are needed.
 
There are times code needs to be repeated a fixed number of times.
This can be based on a count or on the number of elements in a
collection:
 
for index in 10..<40 {
 
  print(index)
 
}
 
for index in stride(from: 10, through: 40, by: 5) {
 
  print(index)
 
}
 

--- Page 111 ---
for name in ["Apple", "Amazon", "Oracle", "Facebook", "Microsoft"]
{
 
  print(name)
 
}
 
In all of these, the for keyword is used, then the name of the variable to
use in the scope, the keyword followed by the source of the values to
assign to the variable. The source could be a range, which we covered
with the switch statement above, a function like stride that can give us
values, or a collection. If the variable is unused, substitute it with an
underscore.
 
Sometimes the code is not run a specific number of times, but, instead,
it is repeated until a logical condition is true. For this while or repeat-
while loops are used. The difference between the two is when the
Boolean expression is checked for conformance. Using while tests the
expression before executing any of the instructions in the scope
whereas the second option executes the instructions before testing.
Add this code, but do not run yet:
 
var testValue = true
 
while !testValue {

--- Page 112 ---
 
  print("running some code")
 
}
 
repeat {
 
  print("running extra code")
 
} while !testValue
 
The exclamation point is a not operator. It reverses the Boolean value
of what follows. Hence, would be true and !true is false. Before
running it, try to determine what will print.
 
The return statement has been shown as the way of returning a value
from a method or calculated property, however it can also be used to
exit the flow in a method that does not return a value.
 
Sometimes, specific conditions must be met before subsequent
statements can be executed. To achieve this, use guard. The guard
command followed by let or var syntax tests if the following optional
variable can be unwrapped and assigned to a variable of the same

--- Page 113 ---
name. A comma delimited list of Boolean expressions to test can also
be used. Here is an example:
 
struct Book {
 
  let author: String?
 
  func promoteAuthor() {
 
      guard let author, !author.isEmpty else { return }
 
      print("\(author) is a great author!")
 
  }
 
}
 
let book = Book(author: "Aaron L Bratcher")
 
book.promoteAuthor()
 
The method in the struct checks if author can be unwrapped into a
variable called author and, additionally, checks that the value is not
empty. A string is a collection of characters so we can use the isEmpty

--- Page 114 ---
property. What would happen if the author were given an empty string
on line eight? An empty string is two quotes with nothing in between
 
The guard statement requires an else clause that must eventually
return. A popular use of the guard statement is to simply check
conditions without unwrapping optionals.
 
Finally, there is the very useful defer command. This is for code that
must be executed before exiting the scope regardless of any other code
that may or may not execute. For instance, if a file is opened must be
closed or a memory intensive object must be released. This is easily
illustrated in this code:
 
struct Book2 {
 
  let author: String?
 
  var bigData: Data?
 
  mutating func promoteAuthor() {
 
      bigData = "This is a large data object".data(using: .utf8)
 
      defer {

--- Page 115 ---
 
bigData = nil
 
        print("big data removed")
 
      }
 
      guard let author, !author.isEmpty else {
 
        print("Author not specified")
 
            return
 
        }
 
        print("\(author) is a great author!")
 
      }
 
}
 
var book2 = Book2(author: "Aaron L Bratcher")
 

--- Page 116 ---
book2.promoteAuthor()
 
First, this is a good use of a mutating method. Inside the method, a
large block of data is assigned to the struct’s property and, unless we
do something to remove it, the data will remain with the Book2 object.
Second, the defer command is introduced. Everything in the defer
command’s scope is executed before exiting the current scope. In this
case the current scope is the method, but it could be the commands in
any of the flow control statements too. Test this by running as
presented here, then run again using an empty string for the author
parameter.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 117 ---
 
Order of operation
 
Without cheating, identify the value of 4 + 8 / 22 - ( 2 * 2 Enter this
code into your playground and see the answer:
 
let x = 4 + 8 / pow(2,2) - (2 * 2)
 
To guarantee the same answer, regardless of environment, a standard
was decided upon long ago. It goes like this: Parenthesis, exponents,
multiplication/division, then addition/subtraction. After these are the
comparison operators like greater than less than greater than or equal
less than or equal <= or equal Using this information, we can break
down the expression into steps. Notice how the operators have a space
on either side:
 
let  parenthesisResult: Decimal = (2 * 2)
 
let step1 = 4 + 8 / pow(2,2) - parenthesisResult
 
let exponentResult = pow(2,2)
 
let step2 = 4 + 8 / exponentResult - parenthesisResult

--- Page 118 ---
 
let divisionResult = 8 / exponentResult
 
let step3 = 4 + divisionResult - parenthesisResult
 
When more than one expression of the same priority exists, they are
evaluated in left to right order.
OceanofPDF.com

great thing about this language is that a little knowledge can be used to
create some great programs. As you use Swift, more of its features will
be discovered. Seeing your dream come to fruition is exhilarating.
 
The next chapter will cover a technique of creating the user interface
using SwiftUI.

Introduction
 
In 2019, at the Worldwide Developers Conference, Apple introduced
the world to a new way of designing the user interface for all its
products — SwiftUI. Every year since then, Apple has enhanced and
expanded on its capabilities. SwiftUI makes it easy to describe how the
interface should look and act. The new framework provides all the
elements necessary to create great-looking apps with minimal work.
What makes it especially powerful is the near-immediate preview of
the interface created by the Swift code for different scenarios that are
bult-in or developer-provided.
OceanofPDF.com

The view
 
Select ContentView in the project navigator so the file shows in the
editor pane. By default, new files have a standard comment at the top

utilized by the rest of the file. Here, the SwiftUI library is imported.
More libraries or modules will be used throughout the book.
 
At last, we see a called and it conforms to a protocol called When
working within SwiftUI, it can be easy to forget that views are structs.
The View protocol has one requirement: a property called body that is
of type View. How do we know this? Hold the command key ( ) and
click on the name of the protocol. Xcode will jump to its definition.

Layout
 
The body can have only one view returned. However, that view can
contain other views. Here, the VStack is returned and it, in turn,
contains Image and Text objects. Below the struct is a #Preview area to
define what is shown in the canvas. Even with these few things in the
file, there is a lot to discuss.
 
The VStack is a way of showing items vertically. Change it to HStack
and the items will be displayed horizontally. Do it now. The order that
the views are given in the stack dictate the order they are presented to
the user. Move the entire line for the text view over the image and see
how the preview changes.
 
Right now, the items are centered. This is the default alignment for
items on the screen and in stacks. There are a couple of ways to shift
the items. One is to add a spacer. Insert Spacer() on the line after the
opening brace of the See how this forces everything over? Move it
after the Text line, like below, and see what happens.
 
HStack {
 
  Text("Hello, world!") 

--- Page 146 ---
 
  Spacer()
 
  Image(systemName: "globe")
 
      .imageScale(.large)
 
      .foregroundStyle(.tint)
 
}
 
Default values for spacing and alignment have been used so far.
Remove the spacer line and add the following parameters to HStack
before the opening brace:
 
(alignment: .bottom, spacing: 25)
 
See how the text and image are now bottom aligned? The default
alignment for stacks is center while the default spacing is eight. Stacks
can also be nested for better control of the layout. To layer elements
one on top of the other, the ZStack can be used.
 
The value for the spacing parameter is in points. A point is a unit of
measure for distance or size. Because screen densities vary, pixels are

--- Page 147 ---
not used. There are 72 points in an inch or about 28.35 points in a
centimeter.
 
The VStack and HStack attempt to layout all elements before
displaying anything. This may be time consuming and give the
application the appearance of not running well. If there are many
elements to display, especially in a scroll view, a better option may be
LazyVStack or These stacks process just enough information to create
the visual elements for the screen and the immediate scrollable area.
OceanofPDF.com

Modifiers
 
The image object has three lines dictating how it is presented. First is
the image object itself using a systemName parameter, and the next
two lines are modifiers.
 
Image(systemName: "globe")
 
   .imageScale(.large)
 
   .foregroundStyle(.tint)
 
Modifiers are a great way to customize objects and finetune the
presentation. If you comment these two lines by highlighting the lines
and pressing command you will see what the image would look like
without these modifiers.
 
The imageScale modifier does not seem to do anything. When it is
commented out, the display appears the same. Where a big difference
is shown is when dynamic type is employed. Remove the comment
slashes and change the imageScale from .large to . and the available
enumerations are displayed. Select small or large and see how it

--- Page 149 ---
changes in the type variants. The type variants can be displayed by
choosing the grid on the canvas toolbar and selecting Dynamic Type
 
In the canvas toolbar, select the Live button on the left. The scale
presets may be useful for some situations; however it is a reasonable
expectation to give a precise size for the image. Remove the
imageScale modifier and replace it with resizable() so the code looks
like this:
 
Image(systemName: "globe")
 
  .resizable()
 
  .foregroundStyle(.tint)
 
The canvas now looks like Figure
 

--- Page 150 ---
 
Figure Resizable image
 
The image is attempting to fill the available space allowed. If you were
to remove the modifier from the bottom of the stack, it will fill even
more space. To fix this, add a frame modifier to the image so the code
looks like this:
 
Image(systemName: "globe")
 
  .resizable()
 
  .foregroundStyle(.tint)
 

--- Page 151 ---
  .frame(width: 50, height: 50) 
 
That is more reasonable. The order modifiers are given is important.
For instance, if you were to move the resizable modifier below the
frame, you would see an error as shown in Figure
 
 
Figure Resizable error
 
Modifiers take the previous object as input, makes modifications, then
generates an output. The output may be a specific SwiftUI object or a
generic view. The frame modifier outputs a generic view; however, the
resizable modifier expects an image as the input. The order is
important because you have a flow from one view to another until you
the final output has been created.
 
Make the text more vibrant by adding these three lines under the Text
object one at a time and watch how the preview changes:
 
.bold()
 
.font(.title)
 
.foregroundStyle(Color(.darkGray))

--- Page 152 ---
 
Try switching the order to see which ones can be directly under the
text object. In this case, they can be in any order. Select the Color
Scheme Variants from the grid button in the canvas toolbar. Play with
the color options to see which are visible on both the light and dark
scheme. Later in the book, custom colors that work with color scheme
variants will be defined.
 
Another way to see how the order affects modifiers is to add a border
at different places. Add these lines of code after the frame:
 
.border(Color.red)
 
.padding(.top, 50)
 
.border(Color.green)
 
Play with the padding values, especially the first one. Valid options are
all, top, bottom, horizontal, vertical, leading, and trailing. These
parameters specify where the padding should be and how much.
Adding borders of different colors is a great technique to see how the
layout is working, though it does not work with a spacer. When you
are done exploring, remove these three lines.
 

--- Page 153 ---
The normal convention of SwiftUI is to put each modifier on its own
line as shown in the code examples.
OceanofPDF.com

Controls
 
Earlier the frame modifier was used to size the image. Let us make
some adjustments so the size is user selectable. To accomplish this, a
property to hold the user-selected size is needed. The collective
property values of an object define its state. In SwiftUI, when a View
property that can change is used, it is prefixed with The image frame
will use this property to specify its size. When using it is a good policy
to make it private.
OceanofPDF.com

Slider
 
First, an interface the user can manipulate for adjusting values is
needed. SwiftUI has a slider control that is perfect for the task.
 
Earlier the text and image objects were put into a horizontal stack. This
looks good, but if a slider is added below the image object, it would
appear to the right of the image. As mentioned earlier, stacks can be

ForEach
 
Used a couple of times already, ForEach uses the source for the
creation of unique views, like the integers as used for the scroll view,
items from an array, or even a dictionary. The trailing closure’s
parameter is the element given from the source. In the example above,
each integer value was passed as the parameter to the closure. The
closure must return a view. Add these two properties to the struct:
 
let destinations = ["Boston", "New York", "Philadelphia", "Chicago"]
 
let trainDestinations = [12: "Boston", 104: "New York", 110:
"Philadelphia", 126: "Chicago"]
 
Wrap the scroll view with a VStack then below the existing
ScrollView add this:
 
ScrollView {
 
  ForEach(destinations, id: \.self) { destination in
 
      Text(destination)
 

--- Page 177 ---
  }
 
}
 
.fixedSize(horizontal: false, vertical: true)
 
.border(Color.green)
 
It is best to be in the practice of using the id parameter of the ForEach
or make sure the source conforms to the identifiable protocol like the
enum did for the picker example. Arrays can use the \.self Here, each
entry in the array is passed to the trailing closure and then printed.
 
Now add this ScrollView inside the VStack below the one you just
added:
 
ScrollView {
 
  ForEach(trainDestinations.sorted(by: <), id: \.key) { train, destination
in
 
     Text("train #\(train) – \(destination)")
 
  }

--- Page 178 ---
 
}
 
.fixedSize(horizontal: false, vertical: true)
 
.border(Color.red)
 
By itself, a dictionary cannot be used as the source. However, by
calling the sorted method, a result is returned that can be used. The id
is using key from each dictionary entry. Remember, a dictionary entry
consists of a key and a value where each key is unique. The key and
value are both passed as parameters to the trailing closure where string
interpolation is used to print the train and destination.
 
Finally, add this scroll view:
 
ScrollView {
 
  ForEach(trainDestinations.keys.sorted(), id: \.self) { train in
 
      trainText(for: train)
 
  }
 

--- Page 179 ---
}
 
.fixedSize(horizontal: false, vertical: true)
 
.border(Color.blue)
 
Once you add this, an error will show: Cannot find ‘trainText’ in This
needs to be provided. Below the closing curly bracket for the body
property, add this:
 
@ViewBuilder
 
func trainText(for train: Int) -> some View {
 
  if let destination = trainDestinations[train] {
 
   Text("train #\(train) – \(destination)")
 
  } else {
 
   EmptyView()
 
  }
 
}

--- Page 180 ---
 
The @ViewBuilder attribute allows the code to be clearer when
creating a view. Not discussed before is the keyword some. When a
method returns something that conforms to a protocol, this keyword
indicates that an object that of this kind will be returned.

List
 
Clear out the file as before, leaving only an EmptyView() for the body
and the preview. In the next chapter the preview and ways to take
better advantage of it will be explored.
 
A list is a scroll view with the ability to enable the user to delete or
reorder entries. It also provides a way of showing the information
hierarchically. Like a ScrollView wrapping a the List is often
combined with a ForEach to provide the items displayed. Enter this
code or copy it from the project in the Learn SwiftUI 3 folder:
 
enum Crust: String {
 
  case thin, handTossed, deepDish, none
 
}
 
enum Sauce: String {
 
  case tomato, white, arrabbiata, none
 
}

--- Page 183 ---
 
enum Topping: String {
 
  case sausage, pepperoni, cheese, onion, mushroom, greenPepper
 
}
 
struct Pizza: Identifiable {
 
  let id = UUID()
 
  var crust: Crust
 
  var sauce: Sauce
 
  var toppings: [Topping]
 
  var toppingsDescription: String {
 
      toppings.map { $0.rawValue }.joined(separator: ", ")
 
  }
 

--- Page 184 ---
  #if DEBUG
 
  static func samplePizzas() -> [Pizza] {
 
      let p1 = Pizza(crust: .handTossed, sauce: .tomato, toppings:
[.cheese, .pepperoni])
 
      let p2 = Pizza(crust: .none, sauce: .white, toppings:
 
[.cheese, .mushroom, .onion, .greenPepper])
 
      let p3 = Pizza(crust: .thin, sauce: .arrabbiata,
 
toppings: [.cheese, .sausage])
 
      return [p1, p2, p3]
 
  }
 
  #endif
 
}
 

--- Page 185 ---
The Identifiable protocol on the Pizza struct indicates there must be a
property called id. This assigns an instance of a unique identifier for
each instance, to the property. The calculated property,
toppingsDescription uses an array method called map. All the
collections have these useful methods: and For more information on
this, look at the documentation in Xcode.
 
A static property can be used without an object instantiation as you
will see below. In this case, it will only be in the compiled product if a
debug scheme is being used.
 
The map function iterates over each entry in the collection, does some
manipulation, and creates a new collection from these items. Normally,
the map function would look like this:
 
toppings.map { topping in
 
  topping.rawValue
 
}
 
However, Swift provides a shortcut for trailing closures that is helpful
when they are short like this. The parameters can be referenced by
zero-based index and used directly, skipping the ‘ in’ syntax.
 

--- Page 186 ---
The next item that is new is the #if DEBUG syntax. Any code between
that and the #endif is only included in the compilation when running in
debug mode. This provides a good way of creating sample data that
can be used for development with the assurance it will not be included
in the final product.
 
Now add the rest of the structs needed for this example:
 
struct OrderType: Identifiable {
 
  let id = UUID()
 
  var name: String
 
  var pizzas: [Pizza]
 
  #if DEBUG
 
  static func sampleOrderTypes(type: String) -> [OrderType] {
 
      return [OrderType(name: type, pizzas: Pizza.samplePizzas())]
 
  }
 

--- Page 187 ---
  #endif
 
}
 
struct Restaurant {
 
  var orderTypes: [OrderType]
 
  #if DEBUG
 
  static func sampleRestaurant() -> Restaurant {
 
      let ot1 = OrderType.sampleOrderTypes(type: "delivered")
 
      let ot2 = OrderType.sampleOrderTypes(type: "pickup")
 
      return Restaurant(orderTypes: ot1 + ot2)
 
  }
 
  #endif
 
}

--- Page 188 ---
 
Note the use of the addition operator for the two arrays in the
Restaurant struct. It is an easy way of creating a single array from two,
or more, source arrays.

      .bold()

Instead of returning some view, this method returns a Text type. This

The NavigationView is needed to allow the navigation to other
screens, to provide a title, and provide a place for toolbar items. Note

view. By default, the title is shown with large type, and it goes to a
smaller one as the user scrolls the content up. Nested ForEach items
allow the hierarchical display of contents within sections.
 
To allow the user to delete or move items, modifiers are used to run
code. As stated before, a property that can be mutated must have the
@State attribute. Replace the restaurant property with this one:
 

--- Page 191 ---
@State private var customers = ["Michael Jackson", "John Lennon",
"Johnny Cash", "Frank Sinatra", "Jimi Hendrix"]

List {
 
  ForEach(customers, id: \.self) { customer in
 
      Text(customer)
 
  }

    indexSet.forEach { index in
 
      customers.remove(at: index)
 
      }
 
  }
 
  .onMove { indices, newOffset in

--- Page 192 ---

  }

.navigationBarTitle(Text("Customers"))
 
.toolbar {
 
  EditButton()
 
}

folder. The list is a simple one. The onDelete and onMove modifiers
execute the code in the trailing closures to perform the work needed.
To delete an entry, swipe the entry to the left and tap the delete button.
To move an entry, press and hold the entry and move it. This will not
work properly in the canvas. Run the project to see it work properly.

.round))
 
        .frame(height: 30)

}
