Custom shape
 
The Apple provided shapes are easy to use and follow a simple pattern.
The name of the shape and any parameters needed to guide the path of
the shape. That path is then filled or stroked at the point of use. These
simple shapes can be stacked using the ZStack or by using the overlay
modifier. When these provided shapes do not work, a mechanism is
available for creating custom shapes. Any struct that conforms to the
SwiftUI Shape protocol can be used as a custom shape. The Shape
protocol requires a path function that takes a CGRect parameter and
returns a Path object. The rect gives a width and height the shape
should fit into. Here is a sample shape:
 
struct Star: Shape {
 
  func path(in rect: CGRect) -> Path {
 
      let point1 = CGPoint(x: rect.midX, y: 0)
 
      let point2 = CGPoint(x: rect.width * 0.62, y: rect.height * 0.375)
 
      let point3 = CGPoint(x: rect.width, y: rect.height * 0.375)
 

--- Page 206 ---
      let point4 = CGPoint(x: rect.width * 0.694, y: rect.height * 0.613)
 
      let point5 = CGPoint(x: rect.width * 0.804, y: rect.height)
 
      let point6 = CGPoint(x: rect.midX, y: rect.height * 0.762)
 
let point7 = CGPoint(x: rect.width * 0.196, y: rect.height)
 
      let point8 = CGPoint(x: rect.width * 0.306, y: rect.height * 0.613)
 
      let point9 = CGPoint(x: 0, y: rect.height * 0.375)
 
      let point10 = CGPoint(x: rect.width * 0.38, y: rect.height * 0.375)
 
      var path = Path()
 
      path.move(to: point1)
 
      path.addLine(to: point2)
 
      path.addLine(to: point3)
 
      path.addLine(to: point4)

--- Page 207 ---
 
      path.addLine(to: point5)
 
      path.addLine(to: point6)
 
      path.addLine(to: point7)
 
      path.addLine(to: point8)
 
      path.addLine(to: point9)
 
      path.addLine(to: point10)
 
      path.closeSubpath()
 
      return path
 
  }
 
}
 
This Star type can be used in a view just like the Apple provided
shapes. The path object has many methods to define the path from

--- Page 208 ---
simple lines, as used here, to more complex. When using lines and arcs
the closeSubpath method needs to be used at the end. See the code
example in the Learn SwiftUI 5 code folder.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 209 ---
 
Effects
 
Effects are modifiers that alter a view’s geometry. Clear your file,
making this the content of the body property:
 
VStack(spacing: 100) {
 
  Text("This is rotated text")
 
    .rotationEffect(Angle(radians: -0.3))
 
  RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: 6)
 
    .stroke(Color.blue)
 
    .frame(width: 30, height: 30)
 
    .overlay {
 
      RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: 6)
 
.stroke(Color.red)

--- Page 210 ---
 
        .frame(width: 30, height: 30)
 
        .offset(x: 5, y: 5)
 
    }
 
  Circle()
 
    .stroke(Color.green)
 
    .frame(width: 30, height: 30)
 
    .scaleEffect(2.0)
 
}
 
The and scaleEffect are the most often used in apps. While the code
shows the effect, the presentation is dull. Animating these effects will
bring life to the app.
 
The key thing to remember when adding animation is that a property
must change so there is something to animate. A state property is

--- Page 211 ---
needed to hold an effect’s value or an indication that an animation is
occurring. Add this property:
 
@State private var offset: Double = 0
 
Then, replace the RoundedRectangle shape with this:
 
Text("Tap Here")
 
  .bold()
 
  .font(.title3)
 
  .padding(8)

    RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: 6)
 
      .stroke(Color.blue, style: StrokeStyle(lineWidth: 2))

  .onTapGesture {
 
    withAnimation(.snappy) {
 
      if offset > 100 {
 
        offset = 0
 
      } else {
 
        offset += 20
 
      }
 
    }
 
  }
 
The onTapGesture modifier is new. With this modifier, anything can be
a button. There is also a Button control that will be covered in the next
chapter. The trailing closure is the code to run when the user taps on
the screen element. Here, the offset is being updated within a
withAnimation function. The optional parameter is the type of

--- Page 213 ---
animation to use. There are several, but the easy-to-use animations are
smooth, snappy, and bouncy. Each of these can also take parameters
for duration which is measured in seconds and extraBounce which
takes a value from -1 to
 
Tapping the button will cause it to smoothly move across the screen,
then back to its starting position. Change the animation to see the
different kinds. To see even more, replace your entire struct with this
code:
 
struct ContentView: View {
 
  @State private var isAnimating = false
 
  @State private var offset: Double = 0
 
  var body: some View {
 
      VStack(spacing: 100) {
 
        Text("This is rotated text")
 
            .rotationEffect(Angle(radians: isAnimating ? -0.3 : 0.3))
 

--- Page 214 ---
        Text("Tap Here")
 
            .bold()
 
            .font(.title3)
 
            .padding(8)
 
            .overlay {
 
              RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: 6)
 
                  .stroke(Color.blue, style: StrokeStyle(lineWidth: 2))
 
            }

                  if offset > 100 {

--- Page 215 ---
 
                    offset = 0
 
                  } else {
 
                    offset += 20
 
                  }
 
              }
 
            }
 
        Circle()
 
.stroke(Color.green)
 
            .frame(width: 30, height: 30)

            .foregroundStyle(Color.orange)

--- Page 216 ---

2.0).repeatForever(autoreverses: true)) {
 
                  isAnimating = true
 
              }
 
            }
 
      }
 
  }
 
}
 

--- Page 217 ---
If you have not seen this kind of syntax before: isAnimating ? -0.3 :
the ? true value> : false value> is called a ternary It tests the value of
the first expression then uses either the true value or false value
accordingly. In Swift, if the test expression is false, the true value is
not evaluated at all. Same for when the test expression is true, the false
value is not evaluated. This is important to know because in some
languages, both true and false expressions are evaluated on a ternary
operator, and it can cause the program to crash.
 
We also see something new with the last Text element. Instead of
displaying a string, it is showing an image. This is a good way of
making the system symbols scale with the surrounding text when the
user is using dynamic sizing.
 
On your own, add a new property and make the spinning arrow work
with the autoreverses parameter set to false without affecting how the
other elements animate. A possible answer is at the end of the chapter.

            .font(.system(size: 100))

}

have variants. By using the base name of the variant can be

animation when the value changes. The options parameter is optional but
used here to make the symbol bounce two times.

--- Page 226 ---
 
Conclusion
 
SwiftUI is a rich and powerful way to express how an interface should
be presented to the user. By using stacks with text, images, controls,
and the plethora of symbols available through SF Symbols, an
interface that is easy to use becomes an impressive tool to achieve the
desired outcome.
 
The next chapter shows how to create reusable elements that can be
used through an app or framework and how to create a theme, so the
product has continuity. There will also be a focus on buttons and the
variety of ways they can be used.
OceanofPDF.com

Introduction
 
In the last chapter, common SwiftUI elements and controls provided
by Apple were introduced. They provide the primitives needed to
create the visual appearance and interactions of apps and libraries.
Application designers combine these stylistically to give a theme
throughout the product, so users have a cohesive experience while
using the app.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 239 ---
 
Structure
 
This chapter includes the following topics:
  
Cards
 
Text styles
 
Buttons
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 240 ---
 
Objectives
 
By the end of this chapter the reader should understand how to create
common elements that can be used throughout the app to promote
faster development and encourage a common theme for a consistent
user experience and how multiple SwiftUI views can be created with
sample data and combined to form more complex screens.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 241 ---
 
Cards
 
When designing an interface, two things are considered: How to make it
look visually appealing and how to make it easily understood by the user.
This is why careful thought is put into what the user sees and, sometimes
even more importantly, what is not shown. Visual arrangement and
grouping are a common way to achieve this. The terms and card are nearly
synonymous to define a rectangular area that shows elements and
controls.
 
Figure 3.1 shows some common card designs. The corners can be sharp or
curved. Sometimes a header is shown to identify the content or have an
icon that may or may not respond to user interaction. A card that can
collapse or expand is also common.
 
 

--- Page 242 ---
Figure Common card designs
 
When a visual element or customized control is used several times, it is
best to define it in a central area so it can be reused without copying the
code and to provide a single place where changes can be made when the
styling needs to change.
 
From the File menu, select New and then Project. Select Framework then
the Next button on the bottom. Enter CommonUI for the Product Use the
same organization identifier as before and verify the language is Swift. No
tests are needed here, however click the Include Documentation Your
project options dialog should look like Figure Click the Next button and
create the project:
 


--- Page 243 ---
 
Figure New project options dialog
 
A library is independent code that can be included into other products. It
allows for focused programming that has a single responsibility. In this
case, to deliver common UI elements. The subsequent window will look
like Figure Note the target CommonUI has an icon that looks like a
toolbox. A library cannot be run by itself. However, because of previews
in SwiftUI, how the elements look, and work can be known.
 
 
Figure Framework project window

--- Page 244 ---
 
Select the CommonUI folder in the project navigator then click the +
symbol on the lower left. Select then select Swift File and click Specify
UI Constants.swift as the file name then click the Create button.
 
This will be used to hold values needed for the elements created. Add this
code:
 
import SwiftUI
 
enum UI {
 
  enum Card {
 
      static let cornerRadius = 6.0
 
  }
 
}
 
When creating constants, it is best to use names that express how the
constant is used. Now create a SwiftUI View file and name it Instead of
clicking the + symbol to create a new file, other options include right
clicking the folder to add the file to and selecting New File… or selecting
the folder and selecting New and then File… from the File menu.

--- Page 245 ---
 
If the file is ever saved in the wrong place, it can be moved in the project
navigator to a different folder. Files can also be rearranged in the same
folder for clarity.
OceanofPDF.com

Making the struct public will allow its use outside of the library. If an

generic Card that will accept any content as shown in the Next to the
struct name is this: View>. This view is using generics. Content is the
new type to be used in the rest of the object definition and it must be of
a View type. The body is a stack that allows the content to go as wide
as it can. The background modifier shows the border. Figure 3.4 shows

the constants instead. This basic shape and ability to handle any
content is very useful. Eventually other decisions need to be made:
  
Should the card integrate a shadow and if so, should the color vary
based on the content?
 
Will the card have a header and is the header optional?
 
Can the header show without a body?
 
Is there any standard iconography and is it user intractable?
 
What color should the background be?

Disclosure card
 
A disclosure card has a header, optionally a sub header, and a button
that enables the user to show or hide the card’s content. This element
will be broken into two separate files.
  
First, let us create a folder to store the card files. In the project
navigator, right-click on Card and select New Group from Name the
new folder After creating the new folder, create a new SwiftUI file
inside it called Replace the struct and preview with this:
 
public struct DisclosureCardHeader: View {
 
  let title: String
 
  let subTitle: String?
 
  @Binding var showingDetails: Bool
 
  public init(title: String, subTitle: String? = nil, showingDetails:
Binding) {
 
      self.title = title

--- Page 253 ---
 
self.subTitle = subTitle
 
      self._showingDetails = showingDetails
 
  }
 
  public var body: some View {
 
      HStack(spacing: 0) {
 
        VStack(alignment: .leading, spacing: 0) {
 
            Text(title)
 
              .font(.headline)
 
            if let subTitle {
 
              Text(subTitle)
 
                  .font(.callout)
 

--- Page 254 ---
                  .foregroundColor(.gray)
 
            }
 
        }
 
        Spacer(minLength: UI.Card.headerIconLeadingSpace)
 
        Image(systemName: "chevron.up")
 
            .frame(width: 36, height: 36)
 
            .rotationEffect(.degrees(showingDetails ? 180 : 0))
 
            .padding(.trailing, -UI.Card.headerPadding)
 
      }
 
      .padding(UI.Card.headerPadding)
 
      .contentShape(Rectangle())
 
      .onTapGesture {

--- Page 255 ---
 
        withAnimation {
 
            showingDetails.toggle()
 
        }
 
      }
 
  }
 
}

The @Binding attribute allows a two-way binding between this view
and its parent. The initializer expects a Binding of type Bool that is
then assigned to the local property’s base value. When multiple
parameters are available that change how the view looks or operates,
multiple previews give the developer a way of verifying the options
work properly. The DisclosureCardHeader file has two previews: One
for when the detail content is to be hidden and one for when it is
shown. Above the canvas, a tab is shown for each preview as shown in
Figure
 
 
Figure Preview tabs
 

--- Page 258 ---
The preview shows the content as expected; however, the padding is
too small. Figure 3.7 shows how the text is too close to the edge:
 
 
Figure Header preview
 
Click on the pushpin to the left of the preview tabs. This will keep the
canvas fixed on the current file while editing another. Select the
constants file and change headerPadding to 8.0. The tap gesture will
not do anything here because a constant is used for the preview.
However, when this is used in the card with a binding, the preview will
work properly. The contentShape modifier will set the enclosed shape
as the tappable area.

      self.subTitle = subTitle
 
      self._showingDetails = State(wrappedValue: showingDetails)
 
      self.detailContent = detailContent()

  public var body: some View {

UI.Card.borderThickness)

UI.Card.cornerRadius))

Note that the DisclosureCardHeader is used in the body. Creating
small views that can be previewed and tested then used in a parent
view is called composition. The rule of threes is a good guide of when
to use this technique. If you have three or more lines of code used
three or more times, then it should be considered for separating out to

--- Page 265 ---
its own view. Also note how the showingDetails state is sent as a
binding. The three previews show the possible uses of the

Text styles
 
The Text element is the way of showing text to the user. In the last
chapter basic styling and font selection was shown with this code:
 
Text("Hello, world!")
 
  .bold()
 
  .font(.title)
 
  .foregroundStyle(Color(.darkGray))
 
Besides bold, italics is also available, and they can both be used at the
same time. Using the built-in fonts gives text that is dynamically sized
for accessibility. The font modifier has even more flexibility to what
font and size is used. In the CommonUI project navigator, select the
top level and from the File menu, select New | Playground. Select the
Blank template and click the Next button. Specify a name and on the
bottom, make sure CommonUI is selected for the Add to option.
Create the playground. After creating the playground, your project
navigator should look like Figure

--- Page 267 ---
 
 
Figure Playground in project
 
An advantage of using a playground in your project is that it will have
access to all the objects contained therein. Replace the contents of the
file with this then run:
 
import SwiftUI
 
import CommonUI
 
import PlaygroundSupport
 
struct TextStyles: View {

--- Page 268 ---
 
  var body: some View {
 
      Card {
 
        Text("Hello, world!")
 
            .bold()
 
            .font(.title)
 
            .foregroundStyle(Color(.darkGray))
 
      }
 
  }
 
}
 
PlaygroundPage.current.setLiveView(TextStyles())
 
Note the import CommonUI statement at the top. The library is a
module that needs to be imported for use. Adding a playground

--- Page 269 ---
provides a great avenue of testing your views at the point of use. Try
adding an italic modifier after the bold to see how it looks. Then
remove bold and in its place add a fontWeight modifier.
 
A couple of other ways of customizing the font is to use one of these
modifier variants:
 
.font(.system(size: 12, weight: .light, design: .serif))
 
.font(.custom("AmericanTypewriter", fixedSize: 24))
 
For the first one, the weight and design are optional. Try each one
without any other modifiers except the color. Valid font weights are:
thin, light, regular, medium, semibold, bold, heavy, and black. Designs
include: default, serif, rounded, and A list of built-in fonts can be found
on Apple’s website:
 
Because adherence to text styles is critical in the development of a
project, it is recommended to have a custom view or Text modifier to
enforce the styles.
 
To create a modifier, add a new SwiftUI file in the CommonUI folder
and name it Add this code:
 
extension Text {

--- Page 270 ---
 
  public func style(_ style: TextStyle, color: Color = .black) -> Text {
 
      font(style.font)
 
        .foregroundStyle(color)
 
  }
 
}
 
Extensions were covered in chapter one. This new function can be
called as a modifier to Text views. Because it returns a Text view,
additional Text modifiers can be applied. For this to work, the
TextStyle type must be defined. Add this
 
public enum TextStyle {
 
  case h1, h2, paragraph, footer
 
  var font: Font {
 
      Font.custom(fontName, size: fontSize, relativeTo: textStyle)

--- Page 271 ---
 
  }
 
  var fontName: String {
 
      switch self {
 
      case .h1:
 
        return "Arial Black"
 
      case .h2:
 
        return "Arial Black"
 
      case .paragraph:
 
        return "Avenir Light"
 
      case .footer:
 
        return "Avenir Medium"
 
      }

Buttons
 
From identifying a favorite item to choosing a course of action when
prompted, buttons are used everywhere in a product. SwiftUI has a
plethora of options for displaying a button and customizing its
appearance. In the project playground, replace the body with this code:
 
Button(action: {
 
  print("hello")
 
}) {
 
  Text("link button")
 
}
 
This is an example of a very basic button. The action is the Swift code
to run when the button is tapped. The trailing closure is the appearance
of the button, and it can be a a combination of these in a stack, or a The
advantage of using a button over an .onTapGesture modifier is that the
appearance of a button can change according to the platform it is being

--- Page 277 ---
run on, it can be disabled, and a specialized styling modifier, can be
applied.
 
In the code above, the normal modifiers can be applied to the text. Wrap
the button in a VStack with a spacing of 24 so that other buttons can be

--- Page 285 ---
 
}
 
  .disabled(loadingResource)
 
This is a colorful button that automatically shows a progress indicator
and becomes disabled when the state property is true. This is good for
two reasons:
  
First it gives immediate feedback to the user that the action was
recognized. When an interface is slow to respond, the user feels the
action was not accepted.
 
Second, disabling the button keeps the user from accidentally tapping
twice when it can be problematic. Remember, you can always stop and
start a playground to reset values.
 
Like text styles, the appearance of the buttons should be standardized to
maintain the theme of the project. Three button views will be created
that can take parameters with a final parameter for the action. Using this
format will make it easier at the point of use. The button styles will look
like Figure The option for showing the progress wheel will be available
for the primary and secondary buttons with the button being inoperative,
even if the button is enabled, when showing the progress wheel.

On your own, create a new SwiftUI view called The view needs to take
a double value called progress. It does not need to be a binding because
this view cannot change the value. The value is 0…1 where 1 is equal to
100%. Four bars will be shown in this fashion:
  
When the percent 0…33 only one bar will show red, the others white.
 
When the percent is 34…66 then two bars will show orange, the others
white.
 
When the percent is 67..<99 then three bars will show yellow, and the
last one as white.
 
Otherwise all four bars will show green.
 
These explained states are shown in Figure

OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 296 ---
 
User interface design
 
There is a cycle when designing and maintaining an app. First, a goal is
created. What is the desired objective? The goal should be simple and
focused. With that goal in mind, an interface is created so the user can
clearly identify the goal and easily see a way to achieve it. Then data is
considered. What needs to be collected from the user? What needs to be
presented to the user? Everything the app does is in support of achieving
this goal.
 
Once an app is created that achieves the goal, the cycle starts all over
again. Either the original goal may be modified, a better way is found to
achieve the same goal, or a new goal that is directly related to the original,
is added. When this cycle is ignored, the focus is lost and the app becomes
confusing to the user.
 
The goal for our first app is to track household chores, including what the
chores are and who is assigned to each of them. Normally chores are
assigned to household members and are designated as daily, weekly,
monthly, or seasonal. There may be variations, but this is sufficient. The
design can be chore focused or household member focused. Figure 4.1
and Figure 4.2 show the concept of how it could look with a focus on
chores and a focus on family members. Figure 4.1 shows the list of chores
in the master view, on the left, and the selected chore in the detail view, on
the right:

--- Page 297 ---
 
 
Figure App concept with chore focus
 
In Figure the master view is similar but with the family members listed.
The detail view shows a simple concept of what could be displayed for the
selected family member:
 
 
Figure App concept with family member focus

--- Page 298 ---
 
These are rough ideas that will need to be fleshed out as progress is made
in the next few chapters. The NavigationSplitView can provide this design
and it adapts itself to the platform being run on. The list can show chores
or family members. A way to add and remove these items is needed. The
detail side can show the appropriate information based on what is
selected. Follow these steps:
  
Create a new project using the iOS App template, in Xcode.
 
On the options page, give it the name Household Chores and choose None
for the storage option. Make sure SwiftUI is selected for the interface.
Tests do not need to be included for this project either.
 
To see the split view in action, replace the ContentView struct with the
following code. This small sample will not be in the downloaded source
code:

struct ContentView: View {
 
  @State private var names = Author.sampleAuthors

--- Page 300 ---
 
  @State private var selectedAuthorId: Author.ID?
 
  var body: some View {
 
      NavigationSplitView {
 
        List(names, selection: $selectedAuthorId) { author in
 
            Text(author.name)
 
            }
 
          .navigationTitle("Split View")
 
      } detail: {
 
if let selectedAuthorId, let author = authorData(from: selectedAuthorId) {
 
            Text(author.name)
 
              .font(.title)
 
        } else {

--- Page 301 ---
 
            Text("Please select an author")
 
        }
 
      }
 
  }
 
  func authorData(from id: UUID) -> Author? {
 
      names.filter({ $0.id == id }).first
 
  }
 
}
 
If you find the preview is not updating as expected, select Clean Build

The NavigationSplitView has two components: The master list and the
detail
 

--- Page 302 ---
Run the sample on multiple simulator devices. For the phone, the master
list will show and tapping on an entry will navigate to a subordinate view.
However, on the iPad or a Plus size iPhone in landscape orientation, the
detail will show next to the master list. On the simulator, the phone
orientation can be changed by clicking the rotate button in the title bar.
When the master list is hidden on the iPad or large iPhone, click the reveal
master button on the upper left.
 
The list used is a variant that includes a selection as a selection may not be
made, an optional is used to store the selected value. This list variant also
allows for multiple selections when a set is used to identify what is
selected, however on the split view a single selection is required.
  
Before going further, a review of how data flows is required. In the last
chapter, in the playground, an isFavorite state property was used across
multiple buttons and an image. All these items updated automatically
when the property value changed. The single property is the source of
truth. It can be read and modified directly and if another view or control
needs to update it, then a binding is used. Bindings can traverse through
multiple layers.
 
With the original concept, when a family member or chore is selected, the
detail of the selected item is shown and can be edited. This means the
specific chore or family member is the source of truth. When a class
becomes the source of truth and it must be monitored for changes like a
state property does, it is marked with

specific items. It will, however, take up all available space so the frame is
used in the sample code to limit the size. The negative padding is to
compensate for the card’s built-in padding for internal elements. Remove
these modifiers to see what it looks like. Try it with differently sized
devices.

struct ContentView: View {
 
  @State private var chores = Chore.sampleChores
 
  @State private var selectedChoreId: Chore.ID?
 
  var body: some View {
 

--- Page 316 ---
      NavigationSplitView(columnVisibility: .constant(.all)) {
 
        List(chores, selection: $selectedChoreId) { chore in
 
            ChoreListItem(chore: chore)
 
        }
 
      } detail: {
 
        if let selectedChoreId {
 
            if let chore = choreData(from: selectedChoreId) {
 
              ChoreView(chore: chore)
 
            }
 
        } else {
 
            Text("Please select a chore")
 
              .style(.h1)
 

--- Page 317 ---
        }
 
      }
 
      .navigationSplitViewStyle(.balanced)
 
  }
 
  private func choreData(from id: UUID) -> Binding? {
 
      guard var filter = chores.filter({ $0.id == id }).first else { return nil }
 
      return Binding(get: { filter }, set: { filter = $0 })
 
  }
 
}

Localization of text
 
Before moving too far into the app’s development, some additional things
need to be discussed. The first is localization. The definition for the Text
element shows this initializer: public init(_ key: LocalizedStringKey, …
Of note is the type used, LocalizedStringKey. When a literal string is
used, then an attempt will be made to localize the text. When a variable is
used, then the value is not localized. To perform a conversion, the literal
string is used as a key to search for content in the string catalog. If no
entry is found, then the literal string is presented.
  
Add a new file of type String Catalog to the project. As Figure 4.6 shows,
the catalog will be initially empty:

can be used to add a new language. Next to the new language a percentage
is shown of how much has been updated. As translations are entered, the
percentage is updated.

Accessibility
 
Another important part of designing an app is to make it as usable as
possible by everyone. In Chapter Learn SwiftUI Basics for Creating a
User the canvas toolbar was highlighted as a way to preview dynamic
type variants. The views should be tested against these variants to
verify that the text is changing size as necessary and that the layout
works.
 
Dynamic type is one aspect of incorporating accessibility into the app.
Another is choice of color. When choosing colors, there are a few
things that need to be remembered:
  
Colors should not be too similar as the user may have difficulty seeing
the difference. This is especially true with greens and blues.
 
Text over a background color should have a high contrast so it is easily
visible. For instance, black text over dark blue is not considered
legible.
 
The significance of information should not be tied to color only. For
instance, if some value has been validated, changing the color from
black to green should not be the only indication. Another visual

--- Page 335 ---
element, like a checkmark, should be used because the user may be
color blind.
 
If the user is visually impaired, voice-over on the device may be used.
It would be wise to get acquainted with it on your iOS device. How to
deploy to a physical phone will be learned in a later chapter. For now,
the tools built-in to Xcode will be used to check the accessibility
properties.
  
To get started, and make sure the preview is up-to-date. Then, on the
canvas toolbar, click the selectable tool followed by the inspectors
toggle button on the top-right of the window.
 
When using the selectable tool, properties of the item selected can be
viewed and changed. Should the developer forget the modifier name
for an element or control, it may be available in the attributes
inspector.
 
The accessibility inspector will show details on any item selected in
code or the canvas. Click on either text element showing on the canvas
or in the code. Figure 4.11 is an example of what the inspector will
show:
 

--- Page 336 ---
 
Figure Accessibility Element
 
The label is what is spoken to the user. The built-in elements and
controls usually provide what is desired. One item that will not is the
image. To see this, add Image(systemName: "plus") to the bottom of
and select the image. The label reads but what if it should say Add
Chore? The accessibility modifier has a label parameter that takes a
Text value. Add this below the image: .accessibility(label: Text("Add
There are other accessibility modifiers for different use cases such as
hint, various traits, and even specifying the order when the default is
not appropriate to the circumstance.
 
After validating this works as expected, remove the image and its
modifier from this view. Update the image in the addButton property
of each split view file to the appropriate label. While this technique is
useful, there are some difficulties. For instance, selecting the image of
the button does not show any information in the inspector.
 

--- Page 337 ---
Another way of checking accessibility is to launch the Accessibility
Inspector app. Run the app on a simulator, then switch to Xcode and
from the Xcode menu, select Open Developer Tools followed by
Accessibility Figure 4.12 shows the inspector app:
 
 
Figure Accessibility Inspector App
 
From the menu on the upper-left select Simulator. Bring the simulator
to the foreground, then on the accessibility inspector, click on the
target button at the upper-right. Now, when you hover the cursor over

--- Page 338 ---
an element on the app it will be highlighted, and the accessibility
inspector will show relevant information. Move it down to the add
button to see the new label.
 
Below the target button are controls that allow the developer to move
between items manually or automatically on the simulator screen. The
items are normally presented in order of top-left to bottom-right. If an
item is not presented in the proper order, then the be used with the
accessibility modifier. The higher the number, the earlier in will be
presented to the user. The default sortPriority is zero.
  
To hear how the element is presented with toggle the speech bubble to
the left of the play controls.
OceanofPDF.com

Introduction
 
In the previous chapter, an app concept was presented, and some
models and views were created to give a proof of concept. As an app
grows and becomes more refined, the structure of the data and the
ways of manipulating it must also be refined. This ensures that not
only the original developer can easily find and follow how the code
works, but also any other developer that needs to alter the code.
 
This chapter will go deeper into data modeling, code design patterns,
and asset management. Some of these items will be applied to the
Household Chores while others will be informational only.

Data modeling
 
Creating efficient data models is an important part of creating an app.
Forethought needs to be applied to each object type and the relationships
between them. Types and names should be meaningful with the data held
and names between types should be consistent. The importance of a
variable or property name was also covered in Chapter
 
Open the Household Chores project and select the Chore file from the
project navigator. It was stated earlier that chores can fall into distinct
frequencies. As these frequencies are known, finite values, an enum can
be used. Also, the chore may be assigned to a known family member or
not assigned at all. An ideal place to use an optional. Add this enum above
the chore class:
 
enum ChoreFrequency: String, CaseIterable, Identifiable {
 
  case daily, weekly, monthly, seasonal
 
  var id: ChoreFrequency { self }
 
}
 

--- Page 347 ---
When a string enum does not have values assigned, the case name is used
as the raw value. Considerations also need to be taken for when a chore is
due or archived. Adjust the class to look like this:
 
@Observable
 
class Chore: Identifiable {
 
  var id = UUID()
 
  var name: String
 
  var location: String
 
  var frequency: ChoreFrequency
 
  var assignedTo: FamilyMember?
 
var isComplete: Bool
 
  var isArchived: Bool
 
  var dueDate: Date
 

--- Page 348 ---
  init(name: String = "", location: String = "", frequency: ChoreFrequency
= .daily, assignedTo: FamilyMember? = nil, isComplete: Bool = false,
isArchived: Bool = false, dueDate: Date = Date()) {
 
      self.name = name
 
      self.location = location
 
      self.frequency = frequency
 
      self.assignedTo = assignedTo
 
      self.isComplete = isComplete
 
      self.isArchived = isArchived
 
      self.dueDate = dueDate
 
  }
 
}
 
The property complete was renamed to isComplete so it reads better as a
sentence, The chore is Similarly, an app may have a Person class with a

--- Page 349 ---
property called A location was also added so similarly named chores, like
pickup floor can be targeted to a specific room.

frequency: .seasonal, assignedTo: maria)

For the FamilyMember class, using an empty string as the default for the
name or using an optional value depends on the expectation. As a name is
always be expected, then an empty string is best. Looking at the class, a
circular problem quickly presents itself. Should the family member have
the list of chores, or should the family member be assigned to the chore?
Or can it be both ways?
 

--- Page 351 ---
Having a family member assigned to the chore is a good example of a
one-to-many relationship. The one family member may be assigned to
many chores while the chore has only one family member assigned to it.
However, for the purpose of displaying the data to the user, it also makes
sense to have an array or set of chores in the FamilyMember class. The
way to have both is shown later in the book.

--- Page 356 ---
 
Hashable objects
 
A hash is an integer derived by a mathematical computation on an
object. The hash, along with an equality test, is used to quickly identify
an object in a collection. A Hashable object is one that can create this
hash value and has an equality test. Most types in Swift and the
Foundation library are already hashable.
 
A Hashable object is required to generate the same hash value within a
given run-instance of the app. To make the FamilyMember class
hashable, add this extension in the bottom of the FamilyMember file:
 
extension FamilyMember: Hashable {
 
  static func == (lhs: FamilyMember, rhs: FamilyMember) -> Bool {
 
      lhs.id == rhs.id
 
  }
 
  func hash(into hasher: inout Hasher) {
 

--- Page 357 ---
      hasher.combine(id)
 
  }
 
}
 
After this has been added, the ChoreView should now be error-free.
However, there is still a compilation error preventing the preview from
working. In the ChoreListItem View, change the second text to this:
 
Text(chore.assignedTo?.name ?? "Unassigned".localized)
 
This may, in turn show a chore that is either assigned or not, a second
preview should be created to view the unassigned option.

chores, select the Chores tab and then select an entry from the list.

Design patterns
 
Design patterns provide a template for how code can be structured
when approaching specific situations. While this book focuses on a
select few, there are many patterns that can be used when developing
an app.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 360 ---
 
Builder
 
The builder pattern uses a class to slowly add or remove items to an
object before it is usable. There is no use of this design pattern in the
Family Chores Using the pizza example from chapter one, here is an
example of the builder in use. Open a new playground and enter the
following:
 
enum Crust {
 
  case thin
 
  case handTossed
 
  case deepDish
 
  case none
 
}
 
enum Sauce {
 

--- Page 361 ---
case tomato, white, arrabbiata, none
 
}
 
enum Size: Int {
 
  case six = 6
 
  case twelve = 12
 
  case eighteen = 18
 
}
 
enum Topping {
 
  case sausage, cheese, onion, mushroom, greenPepper, ham, pineapple
 
}
 
struct Pizza {
 
  var crust: Crust

--- Page 362 ---
 
  var sauce: Sauce
 
  var size: Size
 
  var toppings: Set
 
}
 
class PizzaBuilder {
 
  private(set) var pizza: Pizza
 
  init() {
 
      pizza = Pizza(crust: .none, sauce: .tomato, size: .twelve, toppings:
[])
 
  }
 
  func setSauce(to sauce: Sauce) {
 
      pizza.sauce = sauce
 

--- Page 363 ---
  }
 
  func setSize(to size: Size) {
 
      pizza.size = size
 
  }
 
  func setCrust(to crust: Crust) {
 
      pizza.crust = crust
 
  }
 
  func addToppings(_ toppings: Topping...) {
 
      for topping in toppings {
 
        pizza.toppings.insert(topping)
 
}
 
  }

--- Page 364 ---
 
  func removeToppings(_ toppings: Topping...) {
 
      for topping in toppings {
 
        pizza.toppings.remove(topping)
 
      }
 
  }
 
}
 
let builder = PizzaBuilder()
 
builder.setSize(to: .six)
 
builder.setCrust(to: .handTossed)
 
builder.setSauce(to: .arrabbiata)
 
builder.addToppings(.sausage, .onion, .mushroom)
 

--- Page 365 ---
let pizza = builder.pizza
 
The private(set) var pizza line in the Pizza class creates a property that
is accessible throughout the rest of the module, but only settable from
within the class. The use of this builder class starts with let builder =
From here, the builder object is called to set crust and topping options.
At the end, the builder’s pizza property is copied to a new variable.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 366 ---
 
Factory
 
A factory is great when a specific subclass is required, but the parent
class is used as the type. For instance, a shipyard may sell three kinds
of boats: Sail, motor, and row all with a parent class of The factory
may take a model number and other parameters and return the
appropriate kind of object. Here is a simple example:
 
class Boat {
 
  var length: Int = 16
 
  var weight = 4_000
 
  static func makeBoat(model: Int) -> Boat {
 
      switch model {
 
      case 1: return SailBoat()
 
      case 2: return MotorBoat()
 

--- Page 367 ---
      default: return RowBoat()
 
      }
 
  }
 
}
 
class SailBoat: Boat { }
 
class MotorBoat: Boat { }
 
class RowBoat: Boat { }
 
let boat = Boat.makeBoat(model: 2)
 
A factory is especially useful when multiple properties must be set as
the factory can reliably create the object without the user of the object
needing to know all the logistics. There is no use of this design pattern
in the Family Chores app.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 368 ---
 
Singleton
 
The singleton pattern is used when there is no reason to have multiple
instances of a class. Consider the file manager on the phone. It handles
the files on the device and does not need multiple instances to do so. If
one were to write a data management class that only needed the single
instance, then it could be done using the singleton pattern or the
environment method used before. Here is a singleton example:
 
class GameManager {
 
  static var shared = GameManager()
 
  func saveGame() {
 
      print("data saved")
 
  }
 
}
 
GameManager.shared.saveGame()

--- Page 369 ---
 
The shared property is static, so it is accessible without creating an
instance of the class. It is initialized with an instance of the class so it
can be used freely. Singletons can be useful, but can be problematic
when unit testing classes they are used in. Instead of using the
singleton directly, a protocol that mimics the singleton should be used.
This technique will be shown later in the book.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 370 ---
 
Delegation
 
Delegation is used when a helper, or delegate, object is needed to
execute code or return data. The CommonUI framework can take
advantage of the Singleton and Delegation patterns. Adopting these
patterns will allow the CommonUI framework to be used in multiple
projects, allowing each to give specific details on colors, line
thicknesses, images, and font styles.
 
The following steps will be taken:
  
Create style data structs in CommonUI.
 
Create a protocol in CommonUI for receiving the style data.
 
Create a singleton in CommonUI that will house the delegate property.
 
Adjust CommonUI to use the new style information.
 
Create a class in Household Chores that will provide style information.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 371 ---

A variety of ways can be used to pass style data from text files to
structures. The granularity of which can vary. In this project, structs
will be used to house the style data. The approach works well with the
delegate pattern.

The buttons are a lot more complex, requiring a more complex model.
Yes, even creating styles is about manipulating data and the structure
needs to cover all the possible scenarios and still be efficient. The
original designs had the primary button use a background, the
secondary a border, and the tertiary is only the text. However, the user
of the library may want to change that.
 
Create a new Swift file called ButtonStyle and add this code:
 
import SwiftUI
 
public enum ButtonType: CaseIterable {
 
      case primary, secondary, tertiary
 
}

--- Page 376 ---
will become necessary so the delegate can return information relevant
to the specific button type.
 
The button style must be designed to allow the delegate to choose

perfect place to use optionals.

ButtonBackgroundStyle?, enabledBorderStyle: ButtonBorderStyle?,

Note the properties for both enabled and disabled states.

public struct ButtonLabelStyle {
 
  public let style: Font
 
  public let weight: Font.Weight
 
  public let color: Color

Step 2: Create a protocol
 
The delegate needs a protocol to conform to so the CommonUI
framework can retrieve the style information. In the delegate group,
create a new Swift file called CommonUIDelegate and add this code:
 
public protocol CommonUIDelegate: AnyObject {
 
  func fontStyle(for style: TextStyle) -> FontStyle
 
  func buttonStyle(for type: ButtonType) -> ButtonStyle
 
  var cardStyle: CardStyle { get }
 
}
 
A protocol is a contract. Any class, struct, or enum can normally
conform to the protocol, however here AnyObject is specified. The
AnyObject protocol limits the use of the defined protocol to class
objects. This is especially useful for delegates and will be explained
further down.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 383 ---
 
Step 3: Create a singleton
 
The delegate needs to announce itself with the CommonUI library so it
can be called. A class will be created that will act as a singleton. In the
CommonUI group, create a new Swift file called then add this code:
 
import SwiftUI
 
public class CommonUI {
 
  static public let shared = CommonUI()
 
  public weak var delegate: CommonUIDelegate? {
 
      didSet {
 
        setStyleData()
 
      }
 
  }
 

--- Page 384 ---
  var cardStyle = CardStyle(cornerRadius: 6, bodyPadding: 8,
backgroundColor: .white, borderColor: .black, borderThickness: 1,
headerDividerColor: .gray, headerPadding: 8, titleFont: .headline,
titleColor: .black, subtitleFont: .callout, subtitleColor: .gray)
 
  var fontStyles: [TextStyle: FontStyle] = [:]
 
  var buttonStyles: [ButtonType: ButtonStyle] = [:]
 
  private func setStyleData() {
 
      guard let delegate else { return }
 
      cardStyle = delegate.cardStyle
 
      for textStyle in TextStyle.allCases {
 
        fontStyles[textStyle] = delegate.fontStyle(for: textStyle)
 
      }
 
      for button in ButtonType.allCases {
 
        buttonStyles[button] = delegate.buttonStyle(for: button)

--- Page 385 ---
 
      }
 
  }
 
}
 
To fix the error regarding select the TextStyle files and set the
enumeration to be CaseIterable as shown here:
 
public enum TextStyle: CaseIterable
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 386 ---
 
Memory management
 
Normally, a developer does not have to worry about memory
management. An object is created and takes up space in memory.
When it goes out of scope, the memory used is returned to the
available pool and the object is destroyed. Any references to the object
are tracked so the object and its data will remain in memory until there
are not other references. This is called reference
 
When Object A references Object B and it references Object this is a
circular reference. Neither object can be destroyed because a reference
to it exists. To break this cycle, either one of the references must be
removed or a weak reference must be used, instead. The CommonUI
singleton above has a weak property. This means, that if a class that
conforms to the protocol is given as a reference, this property will not
increase the reference count. Instead, should the class go away, the
delegate property will be set to nil. Using a weak, optional, reference
for delegates is a common way of avoiding memory problems.
 
The cardStyle property is provided with a default value. This allows
the Card element to draw all styling values from the singleton. When
programming, there will always be a choice between two or more
options in obtaining the same goal. Sometimes, it is a matter of where

--- Page 387 ---
the code will be placed or how complex it is. Ideally, no more than two
or three levels of abstraction is used.
 
When a delegate is assigned, the didSet property observer is called. It
calls the setStyleData method which, in turn, calls the delegate to get
style information.
 
By using dictionaries for the font and button styles, the TextStyle and
new ButtonType enumerations can be used as the keys for the
associated style information. If an init method were provided, default
information could also be provided here. It is a trade-off of where the
complexity will reside.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 388 ---

var font: Font {

  let size = CommonUI.shared.fontStyles[self]?.size ?? fontSize
 

--- Page 391 ---

?? textStyle

By using optional coalescing, the singleton is referred to for style
information and if it is not available then the previously used code for
the data is used.
 
The buttons, as exemplified by the structs created for the style data, are
a lot more complex. Each type has its own set of expectations on

allowing the delegate to choose how the buttons will look, they are all
almost the same. Except for the tertiary button not showing the
progress indicator, they are all the same in how they might be
displayed. However, the default style information must also be
considered should a delegate not be used. Using the rule of three,
where if code is repeated three or more times it should be refactored so
it can be written once and reused, it becomes obvious the buttons need
to be refactored.

Apple provides a range of built-in colors. To see the list of colors type in
Color. in a Swift file where SwiftUI has been imported. There are times
when custom colors are desired, instead. These will be created and stored
in an asset catalog.
 
If the Assets folder is not in the project navigator, add a new file of type
Asset Catalog and give the name, When working on an app, it is common
for a color palette to be used. Figure 5.4 shows an example palette. The
palette reflects the brand or the style of the app:
 
 
Figure Color palette
 
When adding color assets, it is best to not give a name that reflects the
color, but, instead, a generic name, that is, or
 
Select Assets in the project navigator, click on the + button on the bottom,
then select Color Name the new color, Figure 5.5 shows the new color

--- Page 415 ---
definition with the Any Appearance option selected, and the attributes
inspector shown:
 
 
Figure Color definition
 
The color definition is set on the bottom of the info panel. The dark color
is usually, though not always, a shade lighter. Set the red, green, and blue
values to 233, 112, and 167 respectively. For the dark option use the
values 246, 142, and 218. With the primary color set, add a new color
named BrandSecondary using the values 77, 77, and 157 for the

--- Page 416 ---
AnyAppearance option. When defining the dark color, click the Show
Color Panel button at the bottom of the inspector. After entering the same
values using the RGB sliders, use the HSV sliders to increase the
brightness to 100%. The appropriate values should be reflected to the
attribute’s inspector.
 
Not all colors are suited to have a dark appearance option. Use the
Appearance selector to select None when only the one color is required.
For cultural considerations, use the Localization option to specify color
definitions in localized copies of the app.
 
To use the color, open the StyleManager file and for the
enabledBackgroundStyle variable in the primary case, change the
gradientColorStart parameter from .blue to This type of safety feature
works for colors and images.
 
Colors should rarely be the sole identifier of important information. Icons,
and accessibility text should combine with color to enhance the user
experience. Multiple colors on-screen should not be so similar that the
meaning might be confused. Text color should contrast any color behind
the text so the text can be easily seen.
OceanofPDF.com

Figure Adding image asset
 

--- Page 418 ---
Once the image is added, the properties can be adjusted. By default,
three size options are shown. 1x is a one-to-one correlation of pixel to
point. The next option is 2x, where the image is twice the original size,
and, finally, 3x for an image that is three times the original size. These
are useful when an image does not scale well, and the image needs to
be manipulated for details at different sizes.
 
When an image is vector-based and can scale easily, select Single
Scale from the Scales option in the inspector.

Introduction
 
In the last chapter, the importance of efficient data models was
described with examples. A few design patterns were introduced with a
focus on singleton and delegate patterns that were applied to
CommonUI and Household Chores. The CommonUI library could be
something shared through GitHub, or similar service, with other team
members or the public at-large. In this chapter Xcode’s ability to work
with source control will be shown and the CommonUI library will be
converted to a Swift Package.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 423 ---
 
Structure
 
This chapter includes the following topics:
  
Source Control
 
Swift Package Manager
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 424 ---
 
Objectives
 
By the end of this chapter the reader should understand how to enable
source control with the project, create an account with GitHub, and
perform basic branching and tagging. The reader should also
understand how to create a Swift Package and use one locally and
from a remote source.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 425 ---
 
Source Control
 
Git has been around since the year 2005. Its speed and ability to
facilitate parallel development has made it the de facto means of
handling source control. Having source control has several benefits:
  
Ease of working with multiple developers.
 
Provides a mechanism for delaying code from being included in the
product until the code is ready.
 
Keeps track of changes.
 
Allows a rollback to previous versions of the code.
 
What makes these benefits possible is the use of branches. Branches
house code separately from each other. One developer could be
working on a branch of code for some files to implement a new feature
while another developer works on bug fixes using a separate branch.
When the feature is complete, the branch is then merged into the
branch designated for the app’s delivery. This merge process is usually
a straight-forward mechanism of getting one branch’s changes,
additions, and deletions of code into another. Even while working

--- Page 426 ---
alone, it is a good practice to use source control, so code is properly
managed, and changes are not lost.
 
When a Git repository is created, it has one branch which is the main
branch. Typically, the main branch represents the code in production.
Other branches are created according to an adopted branch strategy.
Like everything in this book, the strategy illustrated here is one of
several that may be considered. Figure 6.1 shows a simple branching
strategy. See below for an explanation of each point in the figure:
 
 
Figure Branching strategy

--- Page 427 ---
  
Repository is created with main branch.
 
Develop branch is created from main branch. The develop branch is
used while work is done on the next release of the project.
 
Bug fix branch is created from the develop branch to house the code
changes needed to fix the bug. The branch at this step could also be a
simple feature branch or could be a branch used to remove unused
code. Several such branches may be used while preparing for the next
app release.
 
Large feature branch is created. The work could be for the next release
or may be for a future release. Code changes are also committed to the
branch as parts of the feature are completed.
 
Feature or bug fix is complete. Code changes are merged into the
develop branch, normally through the use of a pull request. After the
feature branch is merged, it is deleted.
 
Repeat of number 5 with a different branch. Several branches like this
can be created in parallel. While work can be done on a single file in
multiple branches, it is ill-advised as this can lead to merge conflicts.
 

--- Page 428 ---
All work for the release is complete. No more code is merged into the
develop branch, until the release is complete. Testing is completed
with any bug fixes done through a branch/merge process. When the
release is complete, the code is merged into main, a tag is created, and
the branch is unlocked.
  
The main branch has no special properties. It is just another branch
that is treated differently than the others by how it is used. Just as the
main and develop branches can have branches created from them,
branches can be created from any other branch, too. The goal is to
keep the process as simple as possible while achieving the goals of
creating and updating the project.
 
A tag represents the state of code at a particular commit. Tags normally
follow the versioning convention of When working with libraries, a
major version increment signifies a weighty change has taken place
with several new features or API changes that may or may not be
backward compatible. When incorporating a library with this kind of
update, code changes are likely.
 
A minor increment has new features, bug fixes, and possibly some API
additions, but existing users of the library should be able to use the
library without changing the existing code. A minor release is
considered backward compatible.
 

--- Page 429 ---
A patch represents a bug-fix release with no API changes. A patch, or
point, release is considered backward compatible.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 430 ---

Create Git repository on my Mac will create a repository as the project
is created. There is also an option called New Git Repository in the

"my.email@me.com"
 
pbcopy < ~/.ssh/id_github.pub

Swift Package Manager
 
The Swift Package Manager is a dependency manager. When using a
package from the internet, it too, may have its own set of packages it
depends on to function. Each package uses tags to identify its version
and the version of any dependencies it relies on.
 
A package is a lightweight means of sharing libraries. It consists of
source code, resources, and a manifest file. The manifest file, called
defines the products to be created, the targets for execution or testing,
dependencies, and any resources provided by the package. The
package for CommonUI looks like this:
 
// swift-tools-version: 5.9
 
// The swift-tools-version declares the minimum version of Swift
required to build this package.
 
import PackageDescription
 
let package = Package(
 

--- Page 448 ---
  name: "CommonUI",
 
  platforms: [
 
      .iOS(.v17)
 
  ],
 
products: [
 
      // Products define the executables and libraries a package produces,
making them visible to other packages.
 
      .library(
 
        name: "CommonUI",
 
        targets: ["CommonUI"]),
 
  ],
 
  targets: [
 

--- Page 449 ---
      // Targets are the basic building blocks of a package, defining a
module or a test suite.
 
      // Targets can depend on other targets in this package and products
from dependencies.
 
      .target(
 
        name: "CommonUI"),
 
      .testTarget(
 
        name: "CommonUITests",
 
        dependencies: ["CommonUI"]),
 
  ]
 
)
 
The top comment specifies the version of Swift required to read the
package because the package manifest is Swift, the code should look
familiar. It begins with an import after which the variable instantiation
let package = Package(… occurs. The parameters used for this

--- Page 450 ---
example are the name of the package, an array of platforms it works
on, an array of products, and an array of targets. Note that all strings
are static, not variables.
 
While the package cannot be run directly on a simulator, the SwiftUI
code can be previewed on the canvas and unit tests can be run. Testing
will be covered later in the book.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 451 ---
 
Remote packages
 
The ability to incorporate packages from or other sources, adds great
flexibility to Xcode and project management. Using remote packages
makes it easy to incorporate work done by thousands of developers. When
incorporating the work of others, the terms of the license must be adhered
to.
  
Close all projects in Xcode and open the Household Chores project. In the
project navigator, the CommonUI reference is red and non-expandable.
This is because the CommonUI project was removed from the original
location and cannot be found by Xcode. Click on the reference and hit the
delete key on the keyboard. As shown in Figure click the Household
Chores project in the navigator and select the Package Dependencies tab
on the right panel:
 
 
Figure Package dependencies for project

--- Page 452 ---
 
Use this panel to add top-level packages. Any dependent packages will be
automatically added as necessary. Click the plus button to add a package.
Common packages provided by Apple are available and others that have
been used recently. In the upper-right corner of the dialog, enter the
GitHub URL to your space. It is in the format The list of available
packages is shown in the middle pane. When a file called README.md is
included with the package, its contents are rendered on the third pane. The
README file gives information about the package: What it is, how it
works, and sometimes some information on its images.
 
Select the CommonUI package, click the Add Package button, then in the
confirmation dialog add the package. The list of dependencies is updated
and shows the added package. The dependency rule bears some
explanation. By default, the rule will either show the main branch or Up to
Next Major Version depending on whether tags exist or not on the server.
Using up to next major version is the best practice so any updates to the
package by its developer are least likely to negatively impact the project.
The available rules are as follows:
 
Up to next major Using the tags of the repository, all minor and patch
versions from the set major version are considered. If the base version is
set to 1.0.0, then all available 1.x.x versions are considered.
 
Up to next minor All patch versions from the set major and minor version
are considered. If the base version is set to 1.1.0, then all available 1.1.x
versions are considered.

--- Page 453 ---
 
Exact A specific tagged version is used. This is commonly used when a
release of the project is being created so are no surprises occur as final
testing is made.
 
Range of A given range is considered. Useful when using a subset of
features in the package that are backward compatible and other packages
are also dependent on the same package.
 
The specified branch is used.
 
A given commit is used.
   
When multiple packages are used, it is possible that circular dependencies
will occur. Swift Package Manager considers all the rules, to determine
what version of each package to use. Added packages are shown in the
project navigator at the bottom in the Package Dependencies section. With
the package added, the project can be compiled and run.
 
There may be times the locally cached copy of a package does not match
what is on the server. The cache has become stale. To remedy this, from
File menu in select Packages and then Reset Package After choosing any
of the options from the Packages menu, select Clean Build Folder from
the Product menu.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 454 ---

also a way of separating code into manageable modules that are self-
contained. Using modules provides structure and separation of

Conclusion
 
This chapter introduced the Swift Package Manager, a dependency
management system that uses packages as a means of modularizing
and sharing code and resources. Also taught in this chapter was source
control and the means of making, merging, and deleting branches. Tags
are used to version packages and strongly integrated into the
dependency resolution process.
 
The next chapter introduces SwiftData, a modern way for storing data
onto the device the project is running on.

Several elements are needed to make the app useful. Foremost, the
data needs to be saved, or persisted, after the app has been closed. This
chapter will focus on saving application data directly on the device
using a database and enabling user preferences in the iOS Setting app.

based on how frequently each chore must be completed, and make the

SwiftData
 
A variety of packages exist on GitHub to make it easier to save data on
the user’s device or on the cloud. Examples range from AgileDB to
Realm to YapDatabase, each with its own features and complexity.
Utilizing the new macro system, Apple’s SwiftData provides a means
to easily create, update, retrieve, and delete data from a persistent
storage.
 
To utilize SwiftData and complete the app, many changes need to be
done. In the Chore file:
  
add an statement at the top
 
Add @Model above the class declaration in place of
 
This marks the class as a SwiftData model. By default, all the
properties will be saved, however annotations can be placed in front of
properties to change the behavior of how they are handled. Examples
include specifying the unique identifier with @Relationship to specify
the relationship between two classes, and @Transient to specify a
property that is not stored permanently but is still available for use in
the app.

--- Page 461 ---
 
It is a good practice to specify the unique identifier when using
persisted data as it helps facilitate internal optimizations for the data.
 
In front of the id property add this annotation:
 
@Attribute(.unique)
 
Add Codable to the end of the ChoreFrequency definition. This will
allow objects that use the enumeration to save properly.
  
Make the same changes in the FamilyMember file:
  
Add import the top,
 
Change @Observable to @Model
 
Add the unique attribute to the id property.
 
SwiftData has three layers: The models, a context, and a container. The
models define the data to be stored and, possibly, how they relate to
each other. The context is a space for relationships between models
and data to be managed. The context also provides a space for data to
be read from, saved to, modified, and deleted.

--- Page 462 ---
 
By providing relationship information, SwiftData becomes an object
graph management system.
 
Above the chores property, add @Relationship(deleteRule: .nullify,
inverse:
  
The relationship annotation specifies a one-to-many relationship
between the family member and the chores that may be assigned to the
person. The delete rule, nullify, sets the chore.assignedTo property to
nil for each chore the family member is assigned to.
 
To make data access and updating easy, SwiftUI can now reference the
context as part of the environment. Follow these steps:
  
In the ChoreSplitView file, import SwiftData at the top. Then replace
the @Environment…dataModel line with:
@Environment(\.modelContext) private var
 
With the dataModel no longer being used to get chores, a new way
must be specified to reference them. SwiftData introduces the @Query
macro. By itself or with a filter, the query provides an easy and
powerful way to retrieve and sort data.
 

--- Page 463 ---
Below the new context property add: @Query(sort: \Chore.dueDate)
private var chores:
  
A query can only be used in a SwiftUI view. Without any additional
data, this query returns all chores sorted by the due date. However, a
predicate can be applied as a filter. For instance, it may be beneficial to
have only chores that are not marked as complete, returned for use. To
accomplish this, alter the chores property to look like this:
 
@Query(
 
  filter: #Predicate{ chore in
 
      !chore.isComplete
 
  },
 
  sort: \Chore.dueDate
 
)
 
private var chores: [Chore]
 

--- Page 464 ---
The predicate is using generics to identify the model’s type. Like any
other closure, a value can be passed and used in the body. Swift
provides a shortcut to simplify short code blocks. Instead of specifying
variable names, placeholders can be used. $0 is used for the first
variable, $1 for the second, etcetera. Using this information, the query
above can be rewritten:
 
@Query(
 
  filter: #Predicate{ !$0.isComplete },
 
  sort: \Chore.dueDate
 
)
 
private var chores: [Chore]
 
So new chores can be added, replace the dataModel.chores.append line
with Because the dataModel property was removed, passing it as an
environmental modifier in the preview can also be removed.

Deleting data
 
Due to how our list is shown, the onDelete modifier is unavailable for
use. However, buttons can easily be added to a list item by using the
swipeActions modifier. In the ChoreSplitView file, after the closing
curly bracket for the add this code:
 
.swipeActions(edge: .trailing) {
 
  Button(role: .destructive) {
 
      context.delete(chore)
 
  } label: {
 
      Image(systemName: "trash.fill")
 
  }
 
}
 

--- Page 472 ---
When defining the FamilyMember class as a model, a relationship was
assigned to the chore’s property with a deletion rule to nullify the
assignedTo property in the appropriate chore objects. Alternately, the
cascade rule could have been used to delete all chores assigned to the
family member or deny keeping the family member object from being
deleted at all. For this app, the nullify option works best. In the
MemberSplitView file, add the swipeActions modifier using member
instead of chore in the delete command.

Assigning chores
 
The chore view allows a specific family member to be assigned to the
chore. The next step is to assign a chore to the family member in the
member view. This will enhance our original concept by placing an
add button on the section header.
 
Placing another button will make three separate areas where a button is
used to add an object: and now This makes the perfect time to refactor
and make a separate AddButton view. In the project navigator’s UI
Style group, create a new SwiftUI view called Replace the struct and
preview with this:
 
struct AddButton: View {
 
  let accessibilityText: LocalizedStringKey
 
  let action: () -> Void
 
  init(_ accessibilityText: LocalizedStringKey, action: @escaping () ->
Void) {
 
      self.accessibilityText = accessibilityText

--- Page 474 ---
 
      self.action = action
 
  }
 
  var body: some View {
 
      Button {
 
        action()
 
      } label: {
 
        Image(systemName: "plus.circle.fill")
 
            .resizable()
 
            .tint(Color.green)
 
            .frame(width: 25, height: 25)
 
            .accessibility(label: Text(accessibilityText))
 

--- Page 475 ---
      }
 
  }
 
}
 
#Preview {
 
  AddButton("Tester") { }
 
}
 
In the initializer, note the @escaping clause. If the action was used
immediately, this would not be necessary. A calculation or function
could be performed and then the action called. However, the action
here is saved to a local property and run an arbitrary time. The
compiler must optimize the code for this scenario and the escaping
clause prompts this optimization. Should it be left out, an error will be
shown indicating it must be added.
 
The LocalizationStringKey passed is used in the accessibility label
modifier for the visual impaired. To use the add button in the
MemberView chores section, first add this code below the body
property:

--- Page 476 ---
 
@ViewBuilder
 
var sectionHeader: some View {
 
  HStack {
 
      Text("Chores")
 
      Spacer()
 
      AddButton("Add Chore") {
 
        addingChore.toggle()
 
      }
 
      .popover(isPresented: $addingChore) {
 
        VStack {
 
            Text("Select chore to assign")
 
              .style(.footer, color: .gray)

--- Page 477 ---
 
            List(unassignedChores) { chore in
 
              ChoreListItem(chore: chore)
 
                  .onTapGesture {
 
                    chore.assignedTo = member
 
                    addingChore.toggle()
 
                  }
 
            }
 
        }
 
            .frame(minWidth: 200, minHeight: 300)
 
            .padding()
 
      }
 

--- Page 478 ---
  }
 
}
 
The @ViewBuilder annotation makes it easier to use the SwiftUI code
by processing the UI components and any logical conditions and
automatically returning the result as a View without using a return
statement. It is called a result builder.

addingChore: Bool = The second error, Cannot find

Fix previews
 
The views that depend completely on SwiftData cannot show a
preview without some extra work. A temporary data container can be
created with the sample data and can be used in the preview. In the
project navigator create a new Swift file called At the top, import
SwiftUI and SwiftData. By default, new Swift files import Foundation,
however, sometimes, when other frameworks like SwiftUI are
imported, this line can be deleted. Add this class:
 
class PreviewContainer {
 
  @MainActor
 
  static let container: ModelContainer = {
 
      do {
 
        let configuration = ModelConfiguration(isStoredInMemoryOnly:
true)
 
        let container = try ModelContainer(for: Chore.self,
configurations: configuration)

--- Page 486 ---
 
        for chore in Chore.sampleChores {
 
            container.mainContext.insert(chore)
 
        }
 
        return container
 
      } catch {
 
        fatalError("Cannot create container")
 
      }
 
  }()
 
}
 
The two views, and MemberSplitView need their previews updated to
use this container. To use the sample data, add the modelContainer
modifier to the view being called like this:
 

--- Page 487 ---
ChoreSplitView(membersOrChores: .constant(.chores),
showCompleted: false, showPastDue: false)
 
    .modelContainer(PreviewContainer.container)
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 488 ---
 
Daily chores
 
As the list of chores rarely changes, a mechanism must be added that
will automatically find expired chores, archive them, and create new
chores according to the frequency.
 
Several factors must be considered when using date math: Leap years,
time zones, daylight savings time, and more. To simplify the process, it
is always best to use the built-in methods provided by the operating
system. Follow these steps to get a list of expired chores and renew
them:
  
From this chapter’s End code folder, find the file called
DateExtensions.swift from
HouseholdChores/HouseholdChores/Foundation Extensions folder and
drag it to the Foundation Extensions group of the project navigator.
Make sure the Copy items if needed checkbox is selected.
 
Select ChoreSplitView and add this below the current properties:
 
@Query private var expiredChores: [Chore]
 

--- Page 489 ---
@AppStorage(ChorePreference.lastChoreRenew) var
lastChoreRenew: Int = 0
 
init(membersOrChores: Binding) {
 
  self._membersOrChores = membersOrChores
 
  let now = Date()
 
  let startOfDay = now.startOfDay
 
  let expiredChoresPredicate: Predicate = #Predicate {
 
      !$0.isArchived && $0.dueDate <= startOfDay
 
  }
 
  _expiredChores = Query(filter: expiredChoresPredicate)
 
}
 
The expiredChores query property is overridden by the initializer. Note
how it is not accessed directly, but it is prefixed with an underscore.
Using the underscore allows access directly to the value. This

--- Page 490 ---
technique allows the use of parameters in the query or more complex
predicates. Note that the startOfDay variable is created first and then
used in the predicate. Comparisons to fixed values normally require
this technique. Also, enumerations in predicates are not supported at
this time.
 
After the navigationSplitViewStyle modifier, add this one:
 
.onAppear {
 
  renewChores()
 
}
 
Every time the view is shown, the onAppear is invoked. This is a great
way to run code every time a view appears on-screen. It can be used to
change the value of a state property that causes an animation to start or
run a method, as done here.
 
Add the following methods below the body property:
 
private func renewChores() {
 
  // check if 4 hours have passed since last run

--- Page 491 ---
 
  let lastRenewDate = Date(rawValue: lastChoreRenew)
 
  let elapsedSeconds = abs(lastRenewDate.timeIntervalSinceNow)
 
  guard elapsedSeconds > (60 * 60 * 4) else { return }
 
  for chore in expiredChores {
 
      chore.isArchived = true
 
      for newChore in newChores(from: chore) {
 
        context.insert(newChore)
 
      }
 
  }
 
  lastChoreRenew = Date().rawValue
 
}
 

--- Page 492 ---
private func newChores(from chore: Chore, using now: Date = Date())
-> [Chore] {
 
  var newChores: [Chore] = []
 
  let endOfDay = now.endOfDay
 
  var newDate = chore.dueDate
 
  repeat {
 
      switch chore.frequency {
 
      case .daily:
 
        newDate = newDate.endOfNextDay
 
      case .weekly:
 
        newDate = newDate.endOfNextWeek
 
      case .monthly:
 
        newDate = newDate.endOfNextMonth

--- Page 493 ---
 
case .seasonal:
 
        newDate = newDate.endOfNextQuarter
 
      }
 
      let isArchived = newDate < endOfDay
 
      let newChore = Chore(name: chore.name, location: chore.location,
frequency: chore.frequency, assignedTo: chore.assignedTo,
isArchived: isArchived, dueDate: newDate)
 
      newChores.append(newChore)
 
  } while newDate < endOfDay
 
  return newChores
 
}
  
These methods cycle through the expired chores and create new chores
according to the frequency. If needed, more than one will be created
and archived until a valid one has been created. As chores are created

--- Page 494 ---
and added to the context, the chores query will automatically update
along with the list that it drives.
OceanofPDF.com

Many apps have options available to the user that govern how the app
operates. Sometimes small bits of information need to be stored between
instances of the app. Often a screen is dedicated to setting user-defined
options, but for a single screen app such as the Household Chores app,
these options can be shown in the device’s Setting app.

--- Page 501 ---
 
Integrating user preferences
 
To start the process of using the defined options in the app, open the
ContentView file and add the following two lines below the state
property:
 
@AppStorage("showCompleted") var showCompleted = false
 
@AppStorage("showPastDue") var showPastDue = false
 
The @AppStorage indicator is a property wrapper. Property wrappers
provide a way of easily wrapping code that can be run every time the
property changes or used as a means of changing the property value.
AppStorage allows the program to read and write values to user
defaults. The strings in the parenthesis match the identifier values used
in the preference items of the settings bundle. They indicate the key
used to store and retrieve the user preference values. As the app is run,
these properties act like state properties. When they are updated, the
user preferences are updated and vice versa.
 
In the ContentView file, change the call to ChoreSplitView in the
chores case of the switch statement to
ChoreSplitView(membersOrChores: $membersOrChores,
showCompleted: showCompleted, showPastDue:

--- Page 502 ---
 
In ChoreSplitView update the initializer to accept the additional
parameters and use them as follows:
 
Binding, showCompleted: Bool, showPastDue: Bool) {
 
  self._membersOrChores = membersOrChores
 
  let now = Date()
 
  let endOfDay = now.endOfDay
 
  let startOfDay = now.startOfDay
 
  let chorePredicate = #Predicate {
 
    ($0.dueDate <= endOfDay && !$0.isArchived && !$0.isComplete)
 
    || ($0.dueDate <= endOfDay && showCompleted &&
$0.isComplete)
 
    || ($0.dueDate <= startOfDay && showPastDue &&
!$0.isComplete)

--- Page 503 ---
 
  }
 
  _chores = Query(filter: chorePredicate, sort: \.dueDate)
 
  let expiredChoresPredicate: Predicate = #Predicate {
 
    !$0.isArchived && $0.dueDate <= startOfDay
 
  }
 
  _expiredChores = Query(filter: expiredChoresPredicate)
 
}
 
Like before, the predicate is using local variables to compare against,
and the query property is updated. With these final changes, family
members and chores can be created, and they will be automatically
updated as the app is used and the user preferences honored.
 
Update the preview to accept the new parameters, as the display can
change according to what the parameters are, it would be wise to
provide multiple previews:
 

--- Page 504 ---
