showCompleted: false, showPastDue: false)
 
      .modelContainer(PreviewContainer.container)
 
}
 
#Preview("Completed Chores") {
 
  ChoreSplitView(membersOrChores: .constant(.chores),
showCompleted: true, showPastDue: false)
 
      .modelContainer(PreviewContainer.container)
 
}

  ChoreSplitView(membersOrChores: .constant(.chores),
showCompleted: false, showPastDue: true)
 
      .modelContainer(PreviewContainer.container)

}
 
To make the previews functional, the sample data needs to be dynamic,
meaning the dates cannot be static for the preview to work properly.
With the Completed Chores preview showing, toggle the pin button of
the preview. This will keep the preview pane active and visible while
changing the file to edit. Select the Chore file.
 
The selected preview should not show any chores that are completed
or past due. In the sampleChores property follow these steps:
  
Add a new variable below the family member variables: let lastWeek =
Calendar.current.date(byAdding: .day, value: -7, to: Date()) ??
 
Every time the sample chores are used, this will provide a date seven
days in the past. The byAdding method may fail to provide a valid date
if given invalid parameters, so coalescing operator, provides a concrete
value as a fallback option. This is unlikely to occur.
 
Update the first three chore variables as follows:
 
let c1 = Chore(name: "Pickup", location: "Bedroom", frequency:
.daily, assignedTo: liam, isComplete: true)
 

--- Page 506 ---
let c2 = Chore(name: "Make Bed", location: "Bedroom", frequency:
.daily, assignedTo: liam, isComplete: true)
 
let c3 = Chore(name: "Pickup", location: "Living space", frequency:
.daily, assignedTo: maria, isArchived: true, dueDate: lastWeek)
  
After saving the file, the preview will update. These specific chores
will no longer show. Select the Completed Chores preview and the
first two will now show. Select the last preview and the third chore
will show, but not the first two.
OceanofPDF.com

Conclusion
 
SwiftData provides advanced data handling in apps for all of Apple’s
ecosystem by taking advantage of the Swift language and SwiftUI. By
carefully creating the models and defining the relationships, data can
be easily added, viewed, and manipulated.
 
While an in-app preferences screen is useful for letting the user define
how the app should work, single-screen apps should place their
preferences into the device’s Settings app.

--- Page 510 ---
 
Introduction
 
Charts bring a great visual way of summarizing data to any data-heavy
app. In 2022, Apple introduced Swift Charts. This framework is a great
addition to SwiftUI as a way of defining a variety of charts for use in
an app. It is easy to use and has a lot of flexibility with options to give
stunning results involving little effort.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 511 ---
 
Structure
 
This chapter includes the following topics:
  
Explore Swift Charts
 
Integrating a chart into the Chores app
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 512 ---
 
Objectives
 
By the end of this chapter the reader should understand how to create a
variety of charts using simple principles that define most of the charts.
The reader will also know a variety of modifications to customize the
appearance of the charts.
OceanofPDF.com

Explore Swift Charts
 
Swift charts are tightly coupled with Swift UI, bringing a way to
describe the values that represent the chart along with the principal
way of displaying it. The charting system automatically applies
defaults to present a meaningful chart.
 
To get a grasp of how bar charts operate, follow these steps to explore
them in a playground:
  
In Xcode, create a new playground by selecting New and then
Playground from the File menu. Name it
 
Replace the contents of the file with the following for a clean start that
can be used for any SwiftUI playground:
 
import SwiftUI
 
import PlaygroundSupport
 
struct ChartSample: View {
 

--- Page 514 ---
  var body: some View {
 
  Text("Playground")
 
  }
 
}
 
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 515 ---
 
Bar charts
 
Swift charts uses the concept of a Mark to identify an element to be
displayed. For instance, a BarMark represents a single bar on the chart.
Follow these tseps to create a bar chart:
  
Add import Charts at the top of the file, below the other import
statements, then replace this:
 
Chart {
 
  BarMark(
 
      x: .value("Name", "Liam"),
 
      y: .value("% Complete", 40)
 
  )
 
  BarMark(
 
      x: .value("Name", "Maria"),

--- Page 516 ---
 
      y: .value("% Complete", 60)
 
  )
 
  BarMark(
 
      x: .value("Name", "Amir"),
 
y: .value("% Complete", 50)
 
  )
 
}
 
.frame(width: 500, height: 500)
 
.padding(32)
 
Each mark has an x and y value each of which has two parts: The label
for the value followed by the value itself. The first mark above shows
that Liam completed 40% of his chores and so on. The frame is needed
for the playground, but usually not needed within an app. The chart
should look like Figure

--- Page 517 ---
 
 
Figure 8.1: Sample bar chart
 
To display the bars horizontally, swap the x and y values inside each
While there are only three bars in this chart, it is possible to have
several. Looping through an array by using a ForEach statement allows
an arbitrary number of bars to be charted. Start by creating a structure
to hold the data. Add the following struct above
 
struct GraphData {
 
  let familyMember: String
 
  let percentComplete: Double

--- Page 518 ---
 
  init(_ familyMember: String, _ percentComplete: Double) {
 
    self.familyMember = familyMember
 
    self.percentComplete = percentComplete
 
  }
 
}
 
extension GraphData: Identifiable {
 
  var id: String { familyMember }
 
}
 
By specifying an init with underscores in the place of the parameter
names, data can be initialized without specifying the parameter names.
While used for brevity in this example, the technique should only be
used when the meaning of the parameter values is clear at the point of
use.
 

--- Page 519 ---
Above the body property of the ChartSample struct, create an array of
elements to be displayed in the chart:
 
let memberData = [
 
  GraphData("Liam", 40),
 
  GraphData("Maria", 60),
 
  GraphData("Amir", 50)
 
]
 
Finally, replace the chart with the following:
 
Chart {
 
  ForEach(memberData) { data in
 
      BarMark(
 
        x: .value("% Complete", data.percentComplete),
 
        y: .value("Name", data.familyMember)

--- Page 520 ---
 
      )
 
  }
 
}
  
This example has the x and y values swapped so the bar marks are
displayed horizontally. Note the scale is dynamically adjusted
according to the values being displayed. Change the percent completed
in the memberData to values below 20 to see how the scale adapts.
Sometimes it is best to not have dynamic range. In this chart, the
values represent percentages so a fixed scale of 0…100 would be
ideal. Below the closing brace of the chart, add this modifier:
.chartXScale(domain: If the values were vertical, then the chartYScale
modifier would be used.
OceanofPDF.com

Line graphs
 
Grouping values can also be temporal based instead of String.
Consider a line graph showing the output of solar panels throughout
the day. Measurements are made hourly and an average for the season
is graphed:
  
Replace the GraphData struct and its extension with the following:
 
struct GraphData {
 
  let hour: Date
 
  let kWhGenerated: Double
 
  init(_ hour: Date, _ kWhGenerated: Double) {
 
self.hour = hour
 
      self.kWhGenerated = kWhGenerated
 
  }

--- Page 522 ---
 
}
 
extension GraphData: Identifiable {
 
  var id: Date { hour }
 
}
 
extension Date {
 
  func timeFromString(_ timeString: String) -> Date {
 
      let calendar = Calendar.current
 
      var components = DateComponents()
 
      components.year = calendar.component(.year, from: self)
 
      components.month = calendar.component(.month, from: self)
 
      components.day = calendar.component(.day, from: self)
 

--- Page 523 ---
      let timeParts = timeString.split(separator: ":")
 
      components.hour = Int(timeParts.first ?? "") ?? 0
 
      components.minute = Int(timeParts.last ?? "") ?? 0
 
      return calendar.date(from: components) ?? self
 
  }
 
}
 
let winterAverage = [
 
  GraphData(Date().timeFromString("08:00"), 0.1),
 
  GraphData(Date().timeFromString("09:00"), 0.6),
 
  GraphData(Date().timeFromString("10:00"), 1.1),
 
  GraphData(Date().timeFromString("11:00"), 2.3),
 
  GraphData(Date().timeFromString("12:00"), 3.9),
 

--- Page 524 ---
  GraphData(Date().timeFromString("13:00"), 2.6),
 
  GraphData(Date().timeFromString("14:00"), 1.9),
 
  GraphData(Date().timeFromString("15:00"), 1.2),
 
  GraphData(Date().timeFromString("16:00"), 0.8),
 
  GraphData(Date().timeFromString("17:00"), 0.2),
 
  GraphData(Date().timeFromString("18:00"), 0)
 
]
 
The data represents the average amount of energy generated by some
solar panels at each hour. In practice, an actual timestamp and
measurement would be stored, and an average calculated at display

Another way to chart data proportionally is with a pie chart. Remove

SectorMark(
 
  angle: .value("% of Chores", data.percentOfChores)
 
)
 
Instead of x and y values, an angle is used to represent the percentage
of the pie sector. The SectorMark component also takes innerRadius

--- Page 538 ---
and angularInset parameters. By adding an innerRadius parameter to
the the center of the pie chart is opened to create a donut chart. The
angularInset parameter adds space between each sector. Adjust the
code to match this:
 
SectorMark(
 
  angle: .value("% of Chores", data.percentOfChores),
 
innerRadius: .fixed(100),
 
  angularInset: 2.0
 
)
 
Change the parameter values to see how they affect the chart. The
inner radius can be a fixed number of points as shown above or a ratio
to the size of the graph by using .ratio(n) where n is a percentage from
zero to one. To round out the corners of each sector, add this modifier
after the sector mark:
 
With the center of the chart open, some text can be added as a
background. Add the chartBackground modifier to the chart. Figure
8.5 shows the result:

      let frame = geometry[chartProxy.plotFrame!]

        Text("Chore")
 
        Text("Distribution")
 
      }
 
      .bold()

Integrating a chart into the Chores app
 
With a basic understanding of charts, a bar chart can be added to the
app. Open the Household Chores project and select the Chore file.
When sample data is needed, keeping it with the model can be useful.
However, when it is only for the use of previews, there is a place in the
project it can be placed so the #if DEBUG directive is not needed.
Delete the directive and the sample data so the only thing remaining in
the file is the model.
  
In the project navigator, locate the folder called Preview In the folder,
create a Swift file called Sample Add the following content for a week
of daily chores:
 
extension Chore {
 
  static var sampleChores: [Chore] {
 
      let liam = FamilyMember(name: "Liam")
 
      let maria = FamilyMember(name: "Maria")
 

--- Page 542 ---
      let amir = FamilyMember(name: "Amir")
 
      var chores: [Chore] = []
 
      var isComplete: Bool {
 
        let random = Int.random(in: (0...100))
 
        return random > 30
 
      }
 
      for day in (-7...0) {
 
        let isArchived: Bool
 
        let dueDate: Date
 
        if day == 0 {
 
            dueDate = Date().endOfDay
 
            isArchived = false

--- Page 543 ---
 
        } else {
 
            dueDate = Calendar
 
                    .current
 
                    .date(byAdding: .day, value: day, to: Date())?
 
                    .endOfDay ?? Date()
 
            isArchived = true
 
        }
 
        let liamChore = Chore(
 
            name: "Pickup",
 
            location: "Bedroom",
 
            frequency: .daily,
 
            assignedTo: liam,

--- Page 544 ---
 
            isComplete: isComplete,
 
isArchived: isArchived,
 
            dueDate: dueDate
 
        )
 
        let mariaChore = Chore(
 
            name: "Pickup",
 
            location: "Living room",
 
            frequency: .daily,
 
            assignedTo: maria,
 
            isComplete: isComplete,
 
            isArchived: isArchived,
 

--- Page 545 ---
            dueDate: dueDate
 
        )
 
        let amirChore = Chore(
 
            name: "Dishes",
 
            location: "Kitchen",
 
            frequency: .daily,
 
            assignedTo: amir,
 
            isComplete: isComplete,
 
            isArchived: isArchived,
 
            dueDate: dueDate
 
        )
 
        chores.append(contentsOf: [liamChore, mariaChore, amirChore])

--- Page 546 ---
 
      }
 
      return chores
 
  }
 
}
 
Now whenever sample chores are needed, chores with relevant due
dates are returned. The sample family member data can also be moved
into a new file.

var chart: some View {
 
  Chart(completionRates, id: \.name) { rate in
 
      BarMark(

      )

Text("\(rate.percentComplete, format:
.number.precision(.fractionLength(0)))")
 
                              .foregroundColor(.white)
 
                      }
 
  }
 

--- Page 553 ---
  .chartYScale(domain: 0...100)
 
}
 
func calculateCompletionRates(from chores: [Chore]) ->
[CompletionRate] {
 
  struct Amounts {
 
      var chores = 0
 
      var completed = 0
 
  }
 
  var choreAmounts: [String: Amounts] = [:]
 
  for chore in chores {
 
      let name = chore.assignedTo?.name ?? "Unassigned"
 
      var amounts = choreAmounts[name] ?? Amounts()
 

--- Page 554 ---
      amounts.chores += 1
 
      amounts.completed += chore.isComplete ? 1 : 0
 
      choreAmounts[name] = amounts
 
  }
 
  var completionRates: [CompletionRate] = []
 
  for (name, amounts) in choreAmounts {
 
      let completionRate = CompletionRate(name: name,
percentComplete: Double(amounts.completed) /
Double(amounts.chores) * 100)
 
      completionRates.append(completionRate)
 
  }
 
  return completionRates
 
}

--- Page 555 ---
 
The calculateCompletionRates function accepts an array of chores to
use for calculating and returns an array instead of directly updating the
struct’s completionRates property. Using this approach makes it easy
to add unit tests.
 
Finally, update the preview to pass a constant for the binding and
provide a context for the chores:
 
#Preview {
 
  PercentCompleteChart(showingGraph: .constant(true))
 
      .modelContainer(PreviewContainer.container)
 
}

--- Page 556 ---
 
Figure Bar chart showing percentage of completed chores
  
There should always be a title that clearly identifies what the chart
represents. Note how the view hugs the edge of the display. Seeing
this, it is tempting to add padding. However, never add an external
padding to a view when it designed to be embedded into another view.
Add padding at the point of use.
 
Suppose the design team decides a button should always have at least
sixteen points of padding between it and other elements. If the buttons
were designed to have sixteen points of external padding around it, the

--- Page 557 ---
placement inside a view may work well, but if used at the bottom
corner of a card that has its own internal padding specified, the button
would have too much padding as shown in Figure
 
 
Figure Improperly padded button placed in card
 
Let us now reasonably consider how components will be used and
make sample views to show its use. If enhancements are needed,
default parameters can be utilized so existing uses of the component
will not break or change behavior. Or modifiers can be used to enhance
the component’s utility.

the add a showingChart state property with a default value of

--- Page 560 ---
 
Conclusion
 
Charts give an app’s users a quick and easy-to-understand
representation of underlying data. While only one chart was
implemented, the sheet could show a tab bar with where each tab
shows a different view with its own query, processing method, and
chart.
 
The chore model could have estimated time for completion allowing
for a pie chart that presents the number of hours each family member
has assigned for a period. Or a line chart could be added that plots the
percent of chores completed by each family member by month or
quarter.

iOS development including networking, calling, dropping pins onto
the Maps app, and viewing external websites.

--- Page 563 ---
 
Introduction
 
In this chapter a new app is created that gets its data from an external
source. Also, the MVVM, or Model, View, ViewModel design pattern
will be used. This pattern separates the logic, by use of a view model,
from model data and the view.
 
There is discussion within the Swift community if this pattern is valid
when using SwiftUI, however many companies and individuals still
use it. The city schools app introduces several useful techniques for
creating and structuring a network-based app.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 564 ---
 
Structure
 
This chapter includes the following topics:
  
User interface design of new app
 
Model design
 
MVVM
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 565 ---
 
Objectives
 
By the end of this chapter the reader should understand how to
optionally show visual elements based on device abilities or the state
of the app. The reader will also see how to update an existing package,
create models based on an API response, and use the MVVM pattern
in a SwiftUI app.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 566 ---
 
Interface design
 
The purpose of this app is to give a simple listing of schools with the
option to search. Each entry should have a website listing and the
results of SAT scores. If they do not exist, then the buttons to view
them should be hidden. The app must also provide the ability to view a
map showing each school’s location and call the school, but only if the
user is on a device that can make phone calls. Figure 9.1 gives a rough
sketch of the user interface to design:
 
 

--- Page 567 ---
Figure New York city schools app design
 
The third school does not have any scores to show so the button is
hidden. The last card is also missing the call button to illustrate how
the button is not shown if the device cannot make calls. When the
Scores button is tapped, the app will show a sheet with the scores.

When using the MVVM pattern, most of the logic and actions taken by
the user are handled in the view model. The view model will often hold
the values to be displayed to the user. In this case, the view model will
hold the list of schools and scores. To correlate scores to schools, follow
these steps:

With these changes done, the preview will work again. To make the
buttons function, a mechanism to call the view model’s methods must be
put into place. The delegate pattern is an easy way of accomplishing this.
In the delegate pattern, a separate object is referenced to handle business
logic or return data for use.
 
In the Schools group, create a new swift file called HandlesSchoolButtons
and in the file add the following protocol:

--- Page 591 ---
 
protocol HandlesSchoolButtons {
 
  func showMap(for school: School)
 
  func call(_ school: School)
 
  func showWebsite(for school: School)
 
}
 
The SchoolListViewModel will conform to the protocol and be the
delegate. At the top of the file, add this import statement: import then add
this extension to bottom of the file:
 
extension SchoolListViewModel: HandlesSchoolButtons {
 
  func showMap(for school: School) {
 
      guard let lat = school.lat, let lon = school.lon else { return }
 
      let regionDistance:CLLocationDistance = 10_000
 
      let coordinates = CLLocationCoordinate2DMake(lat, lon)

--- Page 592 ---
 
      let regionSpan = MKCoordinateRegion(center: coordinates,
latitudinalMeters: regionDistance, longitudinalMeters: regionDistance)
 
      let options = [
 
          MKLaunchOptionsMapCenterKey: NSValue(mkCoordinate:
regionSpan.center),
 
          MKLaunchOptionsMapSpanKey: NSValue(mkCoordinateSpan:
regionSpan.span)
 
      ]
 
      let placemark = MKPlacemark(coordinate: coordinates,
addressDictionary: nil)
 
      let mapItem = MKMapItem(placemark: placemark)
 
      mapItem.name = school.schoolName
 
      mapItem.openInMaps(launchOptions: options)
 
  }
 

--- Page 593 ---
  func call(_ school: School) {
 
      guard let url = URL(string: "tel://\(school.phoneNumber)"),
UIApplication.shared.canOpenURL(url)  else { return }
 
      UIApplication.shared.open(url, options: [:], completionHandler: nil)
 
  }
 
  func showWebsite(for school: School) {
 
      guard var ws = school.website else { return }
 
if !ws.starts(with: "http") {
 
        ws = "https://" + ws
 
      }
 
      guard let url = URL(string: ws),
UIApplication.shared.canOpenURL(url) else { return }
 
      UIApplication.shared.open(url, options: [:], completionHandler: nil)
 
  }
 

--- Page 594 ---
}
 
While this book does not get into the specifics of the showMap method
gets the school’s location, creates a marker, then shows it in the maps app
with a region area of 10,000 meters. The other two methods should be
easier to follow. The callSchool method creates a telephonic URL and
attempts to make the call. The final method, attaches the https protocol, if
it is missing, and opens the website if possible.
 
To call the delegate methods, the school entry must have a reference to it.
Not the view model class specifically, but to something that conforms to
the protocol. A way to show the scores as a sheet over the list will also be

func scores(for school: School) -> Scores? {
 
  scores[school.id]
 
}
  
A dictionary lookup is incredibly fast. If the scores were stored in an
array, the app would need to iterate over each entry to find a match. The
dictionary uses a hash and an internal structure to quickly find a match.

        GeometryReader { geometry -> Color in
 
            height = geometry.size.height
 
            return Color.clear

--- Page 623 ---
 
Introduction
 
When an app is developed, enhanced, and altered, subtle bugs can
creep in making the app function in ways it was not designed to. Tests
provide a way of making sure elements are on the screen at the right
time or methods work as expected. Xcode provides several tools for
adding tests and searching for bugs.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 624 ---
 
Structure
 
This chapter includes the following topics:
  
UI tests
 
Unit tests
 
Breakpoints
 
Debugger commands
 
Logging
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 625 ---
 
Objectives
 
By the end of the chapter, the user should understand how to add UI
and unit tests, set breakpoints that can provide information, use the
debugger to show information and use the built-in logging features.
Combined, these tools make it easier to track a bug and reduce the
occurrences of future bugs.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 626 ---
 
Tests
 
There are a few ideologies regarding tests. The first is to manually test
each release. As new features are added to the product, each feature is
thoroughly tested, and the app as a whole is subjected to rudimentary
tests to prove the new features have not broken anything.
Unfortunately, this technique is difficult to use for the rare errors that
may occur while the app is used by the end-users and is time-
consuming. Many of these manual tests can be automated.
 
The second is to do test-driven development. This is the extreme
opposite of the first option. Tests that prove the method, struct, or class
perform as expected are created first with the expectation that all tests
will fail. A minimal amount of code is added to the method so the first
test passes. From there, the same technique is applied to each failing
test until all the tests succeed.
 
The last option is to create the feature, or app, then add the tests. This
usually requires refactoring so the tests can run properly. However, if
coding is done with testing in mind, adding appropriate tests can be
easy.
 

--- Page 627 ---
Tests are not only used to validate responses for all known scenarios,
but also to guard against changes that may accidentally cause a change
to the behavior of the app. As refactoring is done or features are added,
the tests add a level of protection to unwanted behavior. When new
issues are found, the fix should also include a new test to be sure the
new issue does not occur again.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 628 ---

UI Tests are used to validate the flow of an app and that screen elements
are shown, or not, at the appropriate times.

Unit tests
 
Unit tests are small and fast. Each test runs against a known context and
validates the results of the method or state of the context after the method
has returned. Like the previews of a SwiftUI view, there may be multiple
tests on a specific method for each possible context or set of parameters.
  
A good place to start is to validate the models will properly be decoded
from the JSON data. Delete the default NY_City_SchoolsTests file.
 
In the downloaded project code, look inside the End folder. From inside
the NY City Schools/NY City Schools/NY City SchoolsTests folder, drag
the files Schools.json and Scores.json to the NY City SchoolsTests group
of the project navigator. Verify the Copy items if needed checkbox is
checked then click the Finish button.
 
These files contain sample school and corresponding score data, because
not all schools have score data, the scores file is missing the scores for one
of the schools.
 
In the NY City Schools Tests group, create a new Unit Test Case Class file
called
  

--- Page 640 ---
Do this by right-clicking the group and selecting New File… from the
pop-up menu.
 
In the displayed dialog, select the Unit Test Case Class entry and click

target NY City SchoolsTests should be selected. Click
 
If a dialog is presented asking, would you like to configure an Objective-
C bridging header? click Don’t
  
At the top of the file, add NY_City_Schools
 
Importing the app target as testable allows the tests to include the access
of internal object types of the target for use in the tests themselves. The
default access scope of structs, classes, and other objects is internal. This
means the objects are accessible only within the module. By adding the
@testable attribute to the import, these objects can be accessed by the test
code as if they were made public.
 
Remove the default methods and add a new one:
 
func testSchoolModel() throws {

--- Page 641 ---
 
  guard let file = Bundle(for: type(of: self)).url(forResource: "Schools",
withExtension: "json") else {
 
      XCTFail("Cannot read json file")
 
      return
 
  }
 
  let data = try Data(contentsOf: file)
 
  let decoder = JSONDecoder()
 
  decoder.keyDecodingStrategy = .convertFromSnakeCase
 
let schools = try decoder.decode([School].self, from: data)
 
  XCTAssertEqual(schools.count, 6)
 
}
 
Here is a breakdown of what the code is doing:
  

--- Page 642 ---
In the first line, the testing bundle is searched for a file called Schools
with the json extension. If it is not found, then the test is abandoned with a
failure.
 
The data of the file is retrieved and assigned to the data variable.
 
The JSONDecoder is created and a decoding strategy is specified. Entries
in the file like school_name are called snake-case. These are translated
into the camel-case properties of the model.
 
After that, the decoder reads the JSON data and attempts to create the
objects, in this case an array of schools, using the decoding strategy.
 
An assertion, or actual test, is made. Here, the number of entries is
expected to equal six.
  
Run the test by clicking the diamond next to the class or the test method.
It will succeed.
 
To verify the test is valid, change the 6 to a different number and run the
test again. It will appropriately fail. Change the test value back to
 
Using the given test method as a template, make another one on your own
called The test will load from the scores file and creates an array with five
score entries. If you need guidance, look at the code for this chapter in the
GitHub repository.

--- Page 643 ---

Breakpoints
 
Breakpoints are a great tool for validating that code has been reached, to
inspect the call stack when the source of how a method is reached is
unclear, to step through code and verify it is operating as expected, or to
see the values of properties or variables at a specific point. They can even
be used when running tests.
  
To illustrate, open To the left of the editor pane are line numbers. Click on
the 17 to the left of where the code says mapShown = A blue indicator
will Select Test from the Product menu to run all the tests or run the
specific test for the map button.
 
Figure 10.8 shows the code paused at the breakpoint. If the highlighted
line of code is not at the breakpoint, then click the stop button and select
Clean Build Folder from the Product menu and try again. Right-click the
breakpoint to see the available options:
 

--- Page 654 ---
 
Figure Code paused at a breakpoint
 
Below the source code is the Debug toolbar. See Figure
 
 
Figure Debug toolbar
  
Master breakpoint toggle. Selecting this will disable or enable all
breakpoints.
 
Pause and continue execution of code.
 
Step over the next line of code.

Conclusion
 
The techniques shown in this chapter are critical in creating and
maintaining apps of all sizes. Neglecting to create tests may seem like
a time-saver until a change is made that unexpectedly breaks another
part of the app. It is best to be in the habit of either creating the tests
up-front or coding in such a way that adding tests becomes easier.
 
The next chapter will retrieve the school data directly from the
network and improve on decoding the data and the unit testing for the
school model.

Codable models
 
The last chapter introduced some basic testing. Specifically, the model
tests only verified that the appropriate number of elements were
created for each type. While that is helpful, more tests are needed to
validate the properties are populated. Open “ModelTests” and add this
test to the bottom of the “testSchoolModel” method:
 
let firstSchool = try XCTUnwrap(schools.first)
 
XCTAssertNotNil(firstSchool.lat)
 
XCTAssertNotNil(firstSchool.lon)
 
The first property of a collection returns an optional because the
collection may be empty, so no value can be returned. To guarantee
that the firstSchool variable has a value, we attempt to unwrap the
result using the XCTUnwrap method. After that a test is made on the
lat and lon properties to verify they are not nil. Run the test and it will
fail. The try keyword means the XCTUnwrap method could throw an
error instead. Error handling will be covered more in a later section.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 670 ---
 
Decoding
 
The model does not have these properties populated form the
downloaded data. To achieve this, Swift has the Encodable and
Decodable protocols where compliant objects provide the appropriate
methods and enumerations that serialize or deserialize the data. If an
object expects to do both operations, it can adopt the Codable protocol.
 
This app specifically needs to decode text data. The technique was
briefly demonstrated in the last chapter. However, to customize the
deserialization, more changes need to be made. Follow these steps to
decode the school data and extract the necessary properties, including
lat and
  
Select the school model in the project navigator and add the following
code:
 
extension School {
 
  enum CodingKeys: String, CodingKey {
 
      case dbn
 

--- Page 671 ---
      case schoolName = "school_name"
 
      case location
 
case phoneNumber = "phone_number"
 
      case website
 
  }
 
  init(from decoder: Decoder) throws {
 
      let container = try decoder.container(keyedBy: CodingKeys.self)
 
      dbn = try container.decode(String.self, forKey: .dbn)
 
      schoolName = try container.decode(String.self, forKey:
.schoolName)
 
      let location = try container.decode(String.self, forKey: .location)
 
      // extract Lat and Lon
 

--- Page 672 ---
      let regex = "\\((.*?)\\)"
 
      var lat: Double?
 
      var lon: Double?
 
      if let latLonRange = location.range(of: regex, options:
.regularExpression) {
 
        let latLon = String(location[latLonRange])
 
            .replacingOccurrences(of: "(", with: "")
 
            .replacingOccurrences(of: ")", with: "")
 
            .replacingOccurrences(of: " ", with: "")
 
        let values = latLon.split(separator: ",")
 
        if values.count == 2 {
 
            lat = Double(values[0])
 

--- Page 673 ---
            lon = Double(values[1])
 
        }
 
      }
 
      self.lat = lat
 
      self.lon = lon
 
      self.location = location.stripParentheticalText()
 
      self.phoneNumber = try container.decode(String.self, forKey:
.phoneNumber)
 
      let website = try container.decodeIfPresent(String.self, forKey:
.website)
 
      if var website = website {
 
        if !website.starts(with: "https://") && !website.starts(with:
"http://") {
 
website = "https://\(website)"
 

--- Page 674 ---
        }
 
        self.website = website
 
      } else {
 
        self.website = nil
 
      }
 
  }
 
}
 
The CodingKeys enumeration gives a list of keys that match the keys
in the JSON literal. When the key name in the JSON is different than
the case name, the desired key name can be assigned to the case. This
allows short, meaningful, key names when the originating data key
may be much longer or when the names do not reflect the values well.
 
The init method takes a decoder object that has a container. From that
container, each property can be directly extracted, like the school
name, and assigned to the property. Others can be extracted only if
present in the container, like the website further down.

--- Page 675 ---
 
Decoding must specify the data type and the matching key. Like any
class or struct, all the properties must be initialized. Using a regular
expression, the lat and lon are extracted from the location.
 
To get the code to compile, select the String file in the Extensions
group and add this method to the extension:
 
func stripParentheticalText() -> String {
 
    var string = self
 
    if let leftIdx = string.firstIndex(of: "("), let rightIdx =
string.firstIndex(of: ")") {
 
        let sansParens = String(string.prefix(upTo: leftIdx) +
string.suffix(from: string.index(after: rightIdx)))
 
        string = sansParens
 
    }
 
    return string
 

--- Page 676 ---
}
 
Back in the ModelTests file, remove the line
decoder.keyDecodingStrategy = .convertFromSnakeCase from the
testSchoolModel method and run the school model test. It will now
pass.
 
Now add this code to the bottom of the testScoresModel method and
run the test:
 
let firstScore = try XCTUnwrap(scores.first)
 
XCTAssertEqual(firstScore.numTakers, 29)
  
Why is the test failing? There are two possible reasons:
  
The property names do not match the input, even with the snake case
key decoding strategy. The strategy removes the underscore and makes
the next letter an uppercase. That is school_name becomes The other
properties do not match this technique.
 
The value types do not match. All the desired properties in the file are
strings.
 

--- Page 677 ---
To make the scores model work, it needs to be altered and there are a
few ways to consider. An approach like the school model can be done
with the enum and Using this technique, the string values in the file
can be properly converted to integers.
 
However, do the values really need to be converted to integers? As no
calculations are being made and the values are just being displayed,
optional string types will work. By making this change, less work will
be needed to deserialize the data. Make the changes:
  
Change the equality test from the integer 29 to the string
 
Select Scores from the project navigator. Change the value types for
readingAvgScore, and writingAvgScore to Several errors will show
when an attempt is made to build the project.
 
In the mockScores property, where the errors are shown, add quotes
around the numeric values to make them strings.

to In the first Preview, change the passed value to The remaining
places where the properties are referenced need to also be changed.
  
With those changes made, the value type has been addressed. The next
issue for proper deserializing is to match the property names. By right

--- Page 678 ---
submenu. Then each property can be renamed so the snake case key

However, there is an easier way, because there are no conversions

extension Scores {

      case dbn

case readingAvgScore = "sat_critical_reading_avg_score"

--- Page 680 ---
 
Downloading data
 
Numerous ways have been devised to facilitate the management of
networking from an app. Apple has provided frameworks to make it
easier to communicate with other devices and services. When working
with web services, the common modes of interaction fall into these
methods: and The simple downloading of data is commonly done with
a get request.
 
Ideally, each area of an app will be responsible for providing its own
knowledge of the data required and how to manipulate it, while the
networking for the app is done through a common interface. In the
project navigator, create a new Swift file called DownloadManager
and add this code below the existing import Foundation:.
 
import OSLog
 
enum DownloadError: Error {
 
  case invalidUrl
 
  case download

--- Page 681 ---
 
  case parse
 
}
 
protocol ProvidesApiInfo {
 
  var baseUrl: String { get }
 
  var endPoint: String { get }
 
  var decoder: JSONDecoder { get }
 
}
 
The enum identifies specific errors that may be thrown. More on error
handling is discussed later in this chapter. The protocol defines items
needed by objects that will use the download manager. Add the
following code to complete the contents of this file:
 
class DownloadManager {
 
  static let shared = DownloadManager()

--- Page 682 ---
 
  static let subsystem = "Download Manager"
 
let executor = Executor()
 
  let logger = Logger(subsystem: DownloadManager.subsystem,
category: "Get")
 
  private init() {}
 
  func retrieveDataDecodable>(using apiInfo: ProvidesApiInfo) async
throws -> T {
 
      guard let base = URL(string: apiInfo.baseUrl) else {
 
        throw DownloadError.invalidUrl
 
      }
 
      let request = URLRequest(url:
base.appendingPathComponent(apiInfo.endPoint))
 
      let absoluteUrlString = request.url?.absoluteString ?? ""

--- Page 683 ---
 
      logger.info("URL: \(absoluteUrlString)")
 
      return try await executor.run(request, decoder: apiInfo.decoder)
 
  }
 
}
 
struct Executor {
 
  func runDecodable>(_ request: URLRequest, decoder:
JSONDecoder) async throws -> DataType {
 
      let (data, _) = try await URLSession.shared.data(for: request)
 
      do {
 
        return try decoder.decode(DataType.self, from: data)
 
      } catch {
 
        throw DownloadError.parse
 

--- Page 684 ---
      }
 
  }
 
}
 
The private init prevents the class from being instantiated elsewhere.
To use the class, the shared property must be used. You may recall
from Chapter Managing Data and Assets, this is called the Singleton
pattern. Observe the use of the ProvidesApiInfo protocol as a data type
in the retrieveData method. Any object that conforms to the protocol
can be used. This method creates a URLRequest with the provided
information and the attempts to return the data returned by the
executor’s run method. This run method attempts to use a shared
URLSession to get the data and then decode it based on the type
provided through the use of generics..
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 685 ---
 
Generics
 
Generics is a term given to the technique by which a method can be
created that takes a place-holder type so the method can be used across
multiple, compatible, types without unnecessarily adding the same
method for each class.
 
To better understand, suppose a simple function was implemented to
add two integers:
 
func addInt(item1: Int, item2: Int) -> Int {
 
  return item1 + item2
 
}
 
Later, it is realized that the addition of double values is also needed so
a new function is added:
 
func addDouble(item1: Double, item2: Double) -> Double {
 
  return item1 + item2

--- Page 686 ---
 
}
 
These functions are the same, except for the value type. Generics
allows the type to be abstracted and defined at the point of use. So
instead of having two functions, one can be used:
 
func addNumber(item1: T, item2: T) -> T {
 
  return item1 + item2
 
}
 
The T is a placeholder for the type. It does not need to be called It
could be called anything that makes the intent clear. In the run method
above, the placeholder is called Wherever the type is normally used,
the generic placeholder is used instead.
 
Generics can be very useful in simplifying code. In the above addition
examples, only types that are Numeric would be desired, otherwise the
compiler will not know how to add the elements. To limit types
allowed, the function can be altered to look like this:
 
func addNumberNumeric>(item1: T, item2: T) -> T {

--- Page 687 ---
 
  return item1 + item2
 
}
 
Numeric is a protocol identifying the type as a numeric value type.
Depending on the need of the function, many other type limiters can be
used such as or even a custom protocol.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 688 ---
 
Async / await
 
Both the retrieveData and run methods use the keyword This indicates
that the method is intended to be run in the background on a separate
thread. When the method is called, the current thread is suspended
until the asynchronous task is complete. To properly download the
school and score data, follow these steps:
  
In the Schools group, add a new Swift file called DownloadManager +
This is a common naming convention when extending an object to
indicate the object being extended and the nature of the extension. Add
this code:
 
import OSLog
 
extension DownloadManager {
 
  typealias SchoolDownloadResults = Result<[School],
DownloadError>
 
  func downloadSchools() async -> SchoolDownloadResults {
 

--- Page 689 ---
      let logger = Logger(subsystem: DownloadManager.subsystem,
category: "Schools")
 
      do {
 
        let schools: [School] = try await retrieveData(using:
SchoolApiData())
 
        logger.info("schools downloaded")
 
        return .success(schools)
 
      } catch DownloadError.invalidUrl {
 
        logger.error("Invalid URL")
 
        return .failure(.invalidUrl)
 
      } catch DownloadError.parse {
 
        logger.error("Error parsing")
 
        return .failure(.parse)

--- Page 690 ---
 
      } catch {
 
        logger.error("Cannot download")
 
        return .failure(.download)
 
      }
 
  }
 
}
 
struct SchoolApiData: ProvidesApiInfo {
 
  var baseUrl: String { "https://data.cityofnewyork.us/resource/" }
 
  var endPoint: String { "s3k6-pzi2.json" }
 
  var decoder: JSONDecoder { JSONDecoder() }
 
}
 

--- Page 691 ---
This adds a new method to the download manager called It, too, is an
asynchronous method and returns the custom type,
SchoolDownloadResults, that was defined by This return type is a
Result that also utilizes generics. It is an enum for a success or failure
with associated data for each enumeration. Note the line that calls the
retrieveData method of NetworkManager:
 
let schools: [School] = try await retrieveData(using: SchoolApiData())
 
It defines a variable, schools as an array. The keyword await indicates
the code should pause and wait for the data to be returned. Note the
SchoolApiData struct conforms to the ProvidesApiInfo protocol so an
instance of it can be used to retrieve data.
 
The download method is asynchronous, too. Asynchronous methods
call other asynchronous methods. At some point, however, the
asynchronous methods may need to be called from code that is
synchronous.
 
In add this code above the class definition:
 
enum LoadingStatus {
 
   case loading
 

--- Page 692 ---
   case complete
 
   case error
 
}
 
Then, below the logger property add the following:
 
private let downloadManager: DownloadManager
 
private(set) var loadingStatus: LoadingStatus = .loading
 
init(downloadManager: DownloadManager =
DownloadManager.shared) {
 
  self.downloadManager = downloadManager
 
}
 
@MainActor
 
func downloadData() async {
 

--- Page 693 ---
  let schoolResults = await downloadManager.downloadSchools()
 
  var downloadedSchools: [School] = []
 
  var downloadedScores: [SchoolId: Scores] = [:]
 
  var loadingStatus: LoadingStatus = .loading
 
  switch schoolResults {
 
  case .success(let schools):
 
      downloadedSchools = schools
 
      loadingStatus = .complete
 
case .failure(_):
 
      loadingStatus = .error
 
  }
 
  let scoreResults = await downloadManager.downloadScores()

--- Page 694 ---
 
  if case let .success(scores) = scoreResults {
 
      downloadedScores = convertScoresToDict(scores)
 
  }
 
  self.allSchools = downloadedSchools
 
  self.schools = downloadedSchools
 
  self.scores = downloadedScores
 
  self.loadingStatus = loadingStatus
 
}
 
private func convertScoresToDict(_ scores: [Scores]) -> [String:
Scores] {
 
  let scoresDict = scores.reduce(into: [String: Scores]()) {
 
      $0[$1.dbn] = $1
 

--- Page 695 ---
  }
 
  return scoresDict
 
}
 
Storing the download manager as a property, instead of simply using
DownloadManager.shared throughout the code is an example of
dependency injection. It allows for a different download manager
object to be used, especially for testing. Also, using the private(set)
property specifies the property can only be set from within the class,
but is still accessible according to the normal access control rules.
 
@MainActor indicates the method should use the main thread when
interacting with the class properties. Whenever working with
properties that are used in a view, they must be updated on the main
thread. Again, there are two places where await is used so the flow of
the asynchronous code is manageable.
 
In the SchoolList file, under the navigation title modifier, add this

--- Page 699 ---
 
Error handling
 
Several times in the project the use of and throws has been used.
These, along with the Error protocol, are the basis for error handling in
Swift. If a method has throws in the definition, then the method should
either call another method using try or throw an error itself.
 
When trying to call a method that throws, there are a few options on
how to handle it:
  
You can choose to do nothing. If the calling method also throws, then
the error will be propagated up the call chain. The retrieveData
methods of the download manager exhibits this with the last return
statement. If the method throws an error, it will be passed to the
retrieveData caller.
 
Wrap the call and response in do…catch blocks and handle the error
locally. It would look like this:
 
do {
 
  // try something here

--- Page 700 ---
 
} catch {
 
  // handle error here
 
}
 
In the run method of the Executor struct, the try is attempted in the do
block. If an error is caught, the code in the catch block is executed.
With this technique, all errors are handled here, however a specific
error can be caught as shown with the downloading of the schools and
In these methods, specific catch Type> clauses are used to catch
specific types of errors.
 
If the method is returning a value, then use the try? syntax to make the
value optional. This would typically be done outside of blocks and
could look like this: let schools: [School] = try? await
retrieveData(using: SchoolApiData()) { ... the code to be executed is in
the curly braces when a value is successfully returned.
  
To throw errors, an enum is defined that conforms to the Error
protocol. The download manager has DownloadError defined, and the
class throws specific cases of this enum and passes the error in the

--- Page 701 ---
failure cases of the downloads. Like other enumerations, associated
data can also be attached to an enum case.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 707 ---
 
Testing
 
With the networking calls in place, the UI tests are not running with a
known data set. It is possible to update the test for the downloaded
data, but what if the data changes or if the data cannot be downloaded?
Instead, the app can be updated to be aware of a testing status and load
the sample data.
 
In the NY_City_SchoolsApp file, add this extension:
 
extension UIApplication {
 
  public static var isRunningTest: Bool {
 
      @AppStorage("testMode") var runningTest: Bool = false
 
      return runningTest ||
ProcessInfo.processInfo.environment["XCTestConfigurationFilePath"]
!= nil
 
    }
 
}

--- Page 708 ---
 
The user default, testMode is not set any place in the app so it will
always be false when the app is normally run. The UI test will update
it on launch. The environment test can determine that unit tests are
running. Combined, this method will determine if any tests are
running.
 
To update the user default with the UI test:
  
Select NY_City_SchoolsUITests in the project navigator.
 
Add this line of code to the testShowScores method just above the
 
app.launchArguments = ["-testMode", "YES"]
 
By putting a dash at the beginning of the key, the appropriate user
default will be updated with the value that follows. Probably obvious,
YES indicates true and NO would be false. Other items can also be
passed in comma-separated pairs. Using this technique, tests can be
created for different scenarios.
  
Finally, to use the new app property:
  
Select SchoolListViewModel in the project navigator.

--- Page 709 ---
 
Add this code at the beginning of the downloadData method in the
view model:
 
if UIApplication.isRunningTest {
 
  logger.info("TESTING — USING MOCK DATA")
 
  self.schools = School.demoSchools
 
  self.scores = Scores.mockScoreDictionary
 
  self.loadingStatus = .complete
 
  return
 
}
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 710 ---
 
Conclusion
 
Networking is a big part of many programs and libraries. The solution
shown here can be easily adapted for more complex scenarios.
Deserializing can range from a simple decodable struct to providing
coding keys, or a more advanced option that utilizes a decoder
Serializing data complexity when posting to the internet can be the
same.
 
Generics can make code cleaner when used properly. Like and any
other topic it is a tool that should be considered for creating clean,
readable, and easily updated code.
 
Errors should be handled gracefully and not ignored. A simple icon or
message to indicate a problem occurred is usually best. Depending on
the type of application, it may be in a small part of the screen or front
and center when it stops the entire app from functioning.
 
The next chapter will look at running the app on a physical device and
giving it to testers and then making it available to the public.

happenings around the world, New Release and Sessions with the

https://discord.bpbonline.com

Introduction
 
Most of the time an app is created to be sold or given away to the
public at large. The App Store is built-in to all iOS devices, so it is
easy for potential users to find and purchase your app. There is no need
for the developer to set up a merchant account or handle the purchases.
Apple has made the process of making an app available to test users
and releasing to the App Store simple.

When designing an icon, remember that the image will be masked so the

Using physical devices
 
When developing an app, testing on a physical device is critical. The
simulator meets most needs of testing on different size devices;
however, a real device gives a real sense of how the app will function.
This is true for transition and animation speeds, how memory is
handled, and how the views work with accessibility. Follow these steps
to deploy the app to a physical device:
  
Enter an Apple ID into Open Xcode and select Settings from the
Xcode menu. Select the Accounts tab at the top, the plus button on the
bottom, followed by the Apple ID and the appropriate password.
 
The phone itself must be enabled for development use. Unlock the
phone and plug it into the Mac. When prompted, specify the computer
can be trusted.
 
Open Xcode and select Devices and Simulators from the Window
menu. On the left pane a list of devices is shown with the connected
device listed at the top. Select the connected phone. An error may be
shown at the top on how to enable the developer mode on the phone.
In the Settings app of the phone, select Privacy & Scroll to the bottom,

--- Page 718 ---
select Developer and enable it. Restart the phone and unlock it again.
When prompted, turn on developer mode and enter your passcode.
 
With the Apple ID entered and the phone prepped, an app can now be
deployed to the phone. Open the NY City Schools app in Xcode and
select the project from the project navigator followed by the NY City
Schools target in the second panel. At the top select Signing & A team
must be selected. Depending on whether the Apple ID used is
associated with a developer account or not, the options and resulting
signing will vary. Figure 12.2 and Figure 12.3 show the primary
differences:
 
 
Figure Signing panel for Apple ID with no developer account
 

--- Page 719 ---
 
Figure Signing panel for Apple ID with associated developer account
 
When no developer account is created, the team selection includes and
the signing certificate in the iOS pane includes the Apple ID itself.
Whereas, the personal team reference is not included when a developer
account is used, and the signing certificate displays part of a certificate
number.
 
In the project window toolbar, select the physical phone as the target’s
destination. To launch the app, click on the run button in the toolbar, to
the left of the project name, or from the Product menu, select After the
app has compiled and been installed on the phone, what happens next
depends on whether a developer account is being used.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 720 ---

App Store Connect
 
The App Store Connect website is used to manage apps. From
creation, to sending test users, to pricing of the app, to deployment and
updates. Using this website requires an annual fee. Visit
https://developer.apple.com/account and log in using your Apple ID.
Click the Enroll today button. Once payment has been made,
developers have access to do all the items mentioned above plus access
to beta software and operating system releases.

Preparing the app for upload
 
Like the tag version mentioned in Chapter the version of an app is
important. Once a version has been uploaded, subsequent uploads must
have a higher version for each class of minor or major version being
updated.
  
Select the project from the project navigator followed by the NY City
Schools target. When the General tab at the top is selected, the app
category, bundle identifier, and version information is shown in the
Identity group. See Figure
 

--- Page 725 ---
 
Figure App target selected with General tab displayed
 
Select an appropriate category for the app and verify the bundle identifier
is accurate. It must be globally unique across the entire array of available
apps for all the products that may run on Apple devices. If the identifier
needs to be changed, it can be altered on the Signing & Capabilities tab of
the project.
 
The Version field holds the major and minor version numbers, and the
build is comparable to the patch or point release in a package. This must
be set before each upload either manually or through automation so there

--- Page 726 ---
is not a conflict with what is already on Apple’s servers. For the initial
upload, version 1.0 with a build of 0 is fine.
 
In the Xcode toolbar, select Any iOS Device as the destination. From the
Product menu, select Archive. Most likely you will get errors and the
operation will fail. When archiving an app, certain parts of the code or
sample data that are accessible during testing and debugging are no longer
available, nor should they be included in the final build of the app. In the
SchoolListViewModel file, wrap the if line and conditional code with the
#if like this:
 
#if DEBUG
 
if UIApplication.isRunningTest {
 
  logger.info("TESTING — USING MOCK DATA")
 
  self.schools = School.demoSchools
 
  self.scores = Scores.mockScoreDictionary
 
  self.loadingStatus = .complete
 
  return
 

--- Page 727 ---
}
 
#endif
 
This prevents the code inside the macro from being compiled outside of
the debug configuration. Other macros, like #if os(iOS) or #if os(macOS)
to import libraries or execute code specific to an operating system, also
exist.
 
In the same file, change the allSchools property to private var allSchools:
[School] = After this change, find the previews where mock data is used
and wrap the previews with #if After these changes the archive will
succeed and display the window shown in Figure The window can also be
shown by selecting Organizer from the Window menu:
 
 

--- Page 728 ---
Figure Organizer window

is useful if an issue is found before the archive is uploaded or testing
versions have expired. When a version is in production, reports on the left
panel provide valuable information about the app.

Test users
 
When possible, it is always best to have the app tested by other users.
People will often use an app in ways that were unanticipated revealing
bugs or behaviors that are not desirable. To create test users and give
the app out to them, follow these steps:
  
To give the app out for beta testing, select TestFlight on the top. When
a new app, or version, is uploaded, the compliance must be set.
 
Click the Manage button next to Missing Compliance and select the
appropriate encryption algorithm used in the app. In this case it is
None of the
 
The next step is to create a group of testers. Click the plus sign next to
Internal Testing and when prompted, name it Alpha and click The
status next to the app will change to Ready to Test and a new section
below will appear for internal testers. Only users that have been added
under Users and Access can be used for internal testing.
 
External testers can be anyone with a device. Click the plus sign next
to External Testing and create a Beta Testers group. Initially an error
will show under Group Management saying there are not any approved

--- Page 732 ---
builds for beta testing. Click the plus button next to Builds and add the
version that was just uploaded as shown in Figure
 
 
Figure Add build to test dialog
 
After clicking fill in the feedback email testers can use when testing
the app, contact information, and deselect the Sign-in required
checkbox. The contact information and sign-in information are needed
by the team at Apple when validating an app for testing that requires
the user to sign in.
 

--- Page 733 ---
The next screen is a message for testers when downloading this
version of the app on new or fixed items to test.
 
After submitting for review, the build will show with the message,
Waiting for Turnaround time from Apple can range from a couple of
hours to a couple of days, depending on the complexity of the build.
 
Meanwhile, click the plus button next to Testers and select Add New
Enter the email addresses of people to test the build. On subsequent
builds, existing testers can be added. To simplify the process, the email
should match the Apple ID used on the tester’s device. Once the build
is approved, invitations will be sent the registered email addresses.
  
Each tester must download a copy of the TestFlight app to the testing
device. Once installed, the tester can click on the View in TestFlight
button prominently shown in the email. The TestFlight app is
launched, and the user can accept the invitation and install the app.
Beta copies have a default expiration date, but a single build can also
be forcibly expired beforehand. To expire a build through the website,
click the build while on the TestFlight tab then click the Expire Build
button.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 734 ---
 
Submitting to the App Store
 
To submit to the App Store, follow the given steps:
  
After testing is complete, images and an optional video must be
prepared. Run the app in the iPhone 15 Pro Max simulator, or a
simulator device of the same size.
 
Save snapshots from the File menu by selecting Save When ready to
record a video, select Record Screen from the File menu. Perform the
activity to be emphasized then, at the top of the simulator, click the
stop button.
 
The video must run from fifteen to thirty seconds. Open the saved
video in the QuickTime app on the Mac and export to a 1080p file to
get a video at the proper resolution for submission.
 
On the App Store Connect website, select the Distribution tab. Verify
iOS or iPad is selected appropriately. Drag the saved snapshots and
video to the region below the iOS Previews and Screenshots heading.
 
Fill in the required fields and click the plus sign next to Builds and
select the tested build followed by the Done button. Again, uncheck

--- Page 735 ---
the Sign-in required checkbox. For timing, the app can be released
automatically once it is approved or set to be released manually or at a
specified date and time.
 
The Save button can be clicked at any time to save the progress and
returned to later. If the app is for sale, select Pricing and Availability
on the left side to set the price and availability worldwide. When all
the required fields are entered, and the snapshots are uploaded, save
again and then click the Add for Review button. Apple will review the
app to guarantee it matches their standards and, if set, release the app
to the App Store.
OceanofPDF.com

Conclusion
 
The App Store provides a secure way of downloading apps and, for
developers, an easy and safe way of delivering apps to users. Tools
provided by Apple allow for beta testing by ad hoc testers and a
smooth transition to the app store.

HAPTER
13
Make a Generic App
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 738 ---
 
Introduction
 
White label apps are a common way for an application to be used by
multiple organizations, each with specific branding options from
colors to iconography, or even features that can be enabled or disabled.
From airlines to banking apps to drone control, the possibilities for
generic apps are broad and varied. It is important to know how to
easily separate the branding from the app parts.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 739 ---
 
Structure
 
This chapter includes the following topics:
  
Designing the app
 
Create target applications
 
Defining the customizable options
 
Bundles
 
Create the common interface elements
 
Create the primary app
 
Running the app
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 740 ---
 
Objectives
 
By the end of the chapter the reader will understand how to
conceptualize and create a white label app with clear points of
branding defined. The reader will also use and refine skills gained in
previous chapters to deliver the product.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 741 ---
 
Designing the app
 
In this chapter a pizza app will be created for two pizzerias. Others can
be added on-demand. A pizza is a simple food with many options. The
crust can be thin, hand-tossed, or deep dish. Toppings can be generally
separated into meat, cheese, and vegetables or fruit. Often, an order
can include side items like breadsticks, cinnamon rolls, and salads, and
drinks. Like many fast-food restaurants, orders are queued, and a timer
may be applied to each order for tracking efficiency.
 
Because this is a generic app, the colors for certain areas of the app
will be defined by each branded target. The specific pizza sizes,
ingredients, and sides will also be defined by each target. The app
defined here will be simple: A list of active orders with an expected
completion timer and a way of adding new orders. Figure 13.1 shows
the first of the concept screens:
 

--- Page 742 ---
 
Figure 13.1 Order list
 
The Active Orders list provides a list of orders that have been placed
with a countdown progress bar on the bottom of each order. New
orders are added to the bottom of the list. When an order is complete, it
can be deleted. Another screen is used to enter new pizza orders.
Figure 13.2 shows the new order entry screen:
 

--- Page 743 ---
 
Figure 13.2 New pizza design
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 744 ---
 
App structure
 
The app will be broken into multiple bundles to make the process
manageable.
  
Two iOS application targets. More can be added as necessary. Each of
these must hold the customizable options directly.
 
An application library. This library will identify the various screens
and business logic necessary for the app to function.
 
A UI library to define custom elements used on-screen.
 
By breaking the app into in this fashion, new brands can be easily
added and work on different aspects of how the app functions and
interface libraries can be worked on simultaneously.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 745 ---

Defining customizable options
 
A color theme is the first place to start when branding so certain
elements need to be customizable to allow a pizzeria to brand the app:
  
A gradient color for the background would give an immediate
presentation to the user. If the brand does not want a color, the
beginning and ending colors can be white or black for dark mode.
White or black is an oversimplification. Often, the colors used are off-
white and very dark grey. Even text in a professional app is rarely one
hundred percent black.
 
Card customizations were previously covered. For some dimension, a
shadow color could also be specified.
 
The colors used for the progress bar can give a sense of urgency as the
timer runs down.
 
Other areas of customization to consider:
  
Custom images that represent the ingredients.
 

--- Page 749 ---
The number of minutes given for new orders to be completed.
 
The font styles.
 
While most of these have been covered using enumerations and
delegation, this app will get the information directly from the app
bundles. This makes the task of adding a new pizzeria much simpler.
 
Colors should not be referred to by a color name, instead the color
definition should be appropriate to how it is to be used. For instance, if
a color is meant to be used for an error card’s background, then it
should be called Also, using a hierarchy of items in the asset catalog
can make it easier to use the same name in multiple places.
OceanofPDF.com

Bundles
 
A bundle is executable code with any required resources the code depends
on such as assets. Applications, libraries, and packages are examples of
bundles. When working with a project, it is possible to include a file in
multiple bundles.
 
Open the two new folders in the project navigator and select Reveal the
inspectors panel on the right side. In the Target Membership section of the
panel, note how the selected file is included in MamasPizzaApp is
included in the MamasPizza target. See Figure
 
 

--- Page 764 ---
Figure Target membership of file
 
There are times it is convenient to include a file, such as a common class
or asset file, in multiple targets because it is meant to be used in all the
targets. However, there is a danger of sharing too many files across
multiple targets making it difficult to work with code in isolation. To
avoid this, the bulk of this app will be in libraries that are imported into
each app. Follow these steps to create the application and UI libraries:
  
Select the project in the project navigator.
 
Click the plus button below the list of targets and search for See Figure

for Embed in Application to

Frameworks, Libraries, and Embedded Content section.
 
Select the MamasPizza target and add the new framework to the selected
target.
 
Create a new iOS Framework called PizzaUI and select None for the
Embed in Application option.
  
The PizzaApp framework will handle the app flow and logic. The PizzaUI
framework will provide the common components that is styled by each
app target.

public struct CardView>: View {
 
  private let content: Content
 
  private let inError: Bool
 
  public init(inError: Bool = false, @ViewBuilder content: () ->
Content) {
 
      self.content = content()
 
      self.inError = inError
 
  }
 
  public var body: some View {
 
      VStack(alignment: .center, spacing: 0) {
 

--- Page 769 ---
        content
 
            .padding(8)
 
      }
 
.frame(maxWidth: .infinity)
 
      .background(
 
        RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: 8)
 
            .stroke(Color.black,
 
                    lineWidth: 1)
 
            .background(backgroundColor)
 
            .clipShape(RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: 8))
 
            .shadow(color: .gray, radius: 2, x: 0.5, y: 1)
 
      )

--- Page 770 ---
 
  }
 
  var backgroundColor: Color {
 
      if inError {
 
        return Color("Card/Background-Error")
 
      } else {
 
        return Color("Card/Background")
 
      }
 
  }
 
}

        }

      .padding()
 
  }
 
}
 
#Preview("Error") {
 

--- Page 772 ---
  ZStack {
 
      Card(inError: true) {
 
HStack {
 
            Image(systemName: "figure.run")
 
              .foregroundColor(.black)
 
            Text("Body of the card")
 
              .foregroundColor(Color("Text/Normal"))
 
        }
 
      }
 
      .padding()
 
  }
 
}

--- Page 773 ---
  
This mostly mimics what was done with the CommonUI card, but
items like corner radius, or line thickness and color are static. These
can easily be added as part of a data set in the target app’s asset file.
Also note the color is a string, because the asset is not a part of the
library, hence the name must be specified as a case-sensitive string. A
shadow was also added for additional styling.
 
If you see this error on the preview canvas: Active scheme does not
build this file then change the current scheme in the toolbar to PizzaUI
as shown in Figure
 
 
Figure 13.9 Select PizzaUI scheme

--- Page 774 ---

are provided to show each possible state. The preview can also be
shown with multiple text sizes and color schemes for additional quality
checks. Note, the error condition will not show colors specified in the
asset catalog. This will be illustrated later.

Progress bars

Like the card, this will mostly use work done previously with some

public struct ProgressBars: View {
 
  private let progress: Double
 
  private var percent: Int {
 
      Int(progress * 100.0)
 
  }
 
  public init(progress: Double) {
 
      self.progress = progress

--- Page 776 ---
 
  }
 
  public var body: some View {
 
      HStack(spacing: 0) {
 
        ForEach(1...4, id: \.self) { index in
 
            Rectangle()
 
              .fill(segmentColor(segment: index))
 
        }
 
      }
 
      .frame(maxWidth: .infinity)
 
      .padding(4)
 
      .background {
 

--- Page 777 ---
          RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: 4)
 
            .stroke(.gray, lineWidth: 1)
 
            .fill(Color.clear)
 
      }
 
    }
 
  private func segmentColor(segment: Int) -> Color {
 
      switch segment {
 
      case 1:
 
        return percent > 0 ? fillColor : .white
 
      case 2:
 
return percent > 25 ? fillColor : .white
 
      case 3:
 

--- Page 778 ---
        return percent > 50 ? fillColor : .white
 
      case 4:
 
        return percent > 75 ? fillColor : .white
 
      default:
 
        return .white
 
      }
 
  }
 
  private var fillColor: Color {
 
      switch percent {
 
      case 0...25:
 
        return Color("Timer/Progress1")
 
      case 26...50:

--- Page 779 ---
 
        return Color("Timer/Progress2")
 
      case 51...75:
 
        return Color("Timer/Progress3")
 
      case 76...100:
 
        return Color("Timer/Progress4")
 
      default:
 
        return .white
 
      }
 
  }
 
}

This simplified file relies on a new FontUtility class to extract
information from the data set created earlier. Note how the asset
variable is of type Add a new Swift file, name it FontUtility and set the
contents to this:
 
import UIKit
 
import SwiftUI
 
public class FontUtility {
 
  public static let shared = FontUtility()
 
  private let fontInfo: [String: FontInfo]
 
  struct FontInfo: Codable {
 
      let font: String
 
      let size: Double

--- Page 791 ---
 
  }
 
  private init() {
 
      guard let asset = NSDataAsset(name: "Text/Styles") else {
 
        fontInfo = [:]
 
        return
 
      }
 
      do {
 
        fontInfo = try JSONDecoder().decode([String: FontInfo].self,
from: asset.data)
 
      } catch {
 
        fontInfo = [:]
 
      }

--- Page 792 ---
 
  }
 
  func fontInfo(for style: TextStyle) -> FontInfo {
 
      let info = fontInfo[style.rawValue.capitalized] ?? FontInfo(font:
"Avenir Light", size: 36)
 
      return info
 
  }
 
  public func colorDefinition(for textColor: TextColor) -> Color {
 
      return Color("Text/\(textColor.rawValue.capitalized)")
 
  }
 
}
 
The FontUtility class is a singleton that gathers the font information
from the main bundle and caches it for use. Note the private init forces
the use of the shared property. When instantiated, the first line in the
init attempts to read from the data asset called Text/Styles from the

--- Page 793 ---
main bundle. If successful, then the font information is decoded and
locally stored. When using the NSDataAsset class, UIKit must be
imported.
 
The fontInfo method takes a text style and searches for its name in the
fontInfo dictionary and returns the corresponding struct. If it is not
found, a default struct is returned.
OceanofPDF.com

import Foundation
 
import Observation
 
public enum Cheese: String, CaseIterable {
 
  case none = "No cheese"
 
  case light = "Light cheese"
 
  case normal = "Normal cheese"
 
  case extra = "Extra cheese"
 
}
 

--- Page 796 ---
@Observable
 
public class Order: Identifiable {
 
  public let id = UUID()
 
  var entryTime: Date
 
  var crustStyle: String
 
  var crustSize: String
 
  var cheese: Cheese
 
  var meat: [String]
 
  var veggies: [String]
 
  public init(entryTime: Date = .now, crustStyle: String = "", crustSize:
String = "", cheese: Cheese = .normal, meat: [String] = [], veggies:
[String] = []) {
 
self.entryTime = entryTime

--- Page 797 ---
 
      self.crustStyle = crustStyle
 
      self.crustSize = crustSize
 
      self.cheese = cheese
 
      self.meat = meat
 
      self.veggies = veggies
 
  }
 
  var toppings: String {
 
      let toppings: [String] = meat + veggies
 
      return toppings.joined(separator: ", ")
 
  }
 
}
 
#if DEBUG

--- Page 798 ---
 
extension Order {
 
  static var mock: [Order] {
 
      let order1 = Order(entryTime: Date.now, crustStyle: "Hand
Tossed", crustSize: "12\"", cheese: .normal, meat: ["Ham"], veggies:
["Onion", "Pineapple"])
 
      let order2 = Order(entryTime: Date.now, crustStyle: "Deep Dish",
crustSize: "16\"", cheese: .light, meat: ["Pepperoni", "Sausage"],
veggies: ["Onion", "Mushroom", "Green Pepper", "Black Olive"])
 
      let order3 = Order(entryTime: .now, crustStyle: "Hand Tossed",
crustSize:"18\"", cheese: .extra, meat: [], veggies: [])
 
      return [order1, order2, order3]
 
  }
 
}
 
#endif
 

--- Page 799 ---
The Order class represents a single pizza to be made. Some items of
note:
  
The Cheese enum is utilizing the CaseIterable protocol. This protocol
provides an allCases property to the enum that returns an array of all
the cases. A very handy feature when showing enumerations to the
user in a list, tab bar, and so on.
 
The toppings property uses the array joined method to take the
elements of the array and combine them into a String with the
provided separator.
OceanofPDF.com

Order view
 
The order view shows the specifics of an order and animates the timer.
Note how a common timer is used to determine the remaining time.
The onReceive modifier to the card is called when a published item’s
value changes. Follow these steps to implement the order view:
  
Add a new SwiftUI file into the Orders group and call it At the top,
below the import SwiftUI statement, add import Almost immediately
an error will be shown saying PizzaUI is not known or other
compilation errors if an attempt to build is made. To fix this, select the
project in the project navigator followed by selecting PizzaApp in the
list of targets. In the Frameworks and Libraries section, add the
PizzaUI framework.
 
Select OrderView in the project navigator and set the rest of the
content to the following:
 
struct OrderView: View {
 
  let order: Order
 
  @State var percentRemaining: Double

--- Page 801 ---
 
  init(order: Order) {
 
      self.order = order
 
      self._percentRemaining = .init(initialValue: 100.0)
 
  }
 
    var body: some View {
 
      Card(inError: percentRemaining < 0.25) {
 
          HStack(spacing: 0) {
 
            HStack(alignment: .top, spacing: 0) {
 
                Text(order.crustSize)
 
                  .style(.headline)
 
                VStack(alignment: .leading, spacing: 4) {
 

--- Page 802 ---
                  Text(order.crustStyle)
 
                      .style(.headline)
 
                  Text(order.toppings)
 
                      .style(.body, color: .subtle)
 
                  Text(order.cheese.rawValue.localized)
 
                      .style(.body, color: .subtle)
 
                  ProgressBars(progress: percentRemaining)
 
                      .frame(height: 20)
 
}
 
                .padding(.leading, 16)
 
            }
 
            Spacer()
 

--- Page 803 ---
          }
 
      }
 
      .onReceive(AppUtility.shared.timer) { currentTime in
 
          let elapsed = currentTime.timeIntervalSince(order.entryTime)
 
          let percentRemaining = 1 - elapsed /
AppUtility.shared.orderTime
 
          self.percentRemaining = percentRemaining
 
      }
 
    }
 
}

The property orderTime is the amount of time, in seconds, an order

public struct OrderList: View {

  public init(orders: [Order] = []) {

--- Page 812 ---
 
self._orders = .init(initialValue: orders)
 
  }
 
    public var body: some View {
 
      NavigationView {
 
          List {
 
            ForEach(orders) { order in
 
                OrderView(order: order)
 
                  .listRowSeparator(.hidden)
 
                  .listRowBackground(Color.clear)
 
            }
 
            .onDelete { indexSet in
 

--- Page 813 ---
                orders.remove(atOffsets: indexSet)
 
            }
 
          }
 
          .padding(.horizontal, -16)
 
          .toolbar {
 
            AddButton("Add Order") {
 
                showSheet = true
 
            }
 
          }
 
          .navigationTitle("Orders".localized)
 
      }
 
      .sheet(isPresented: $showSheet) {
 

--- Page 814 ---
          AddOrder(showingSheet: $showSheet, addOrderDelegate: self)
 
            .interactiveDismissDisabled(true)
 
      }
 
    }
 
}
 
extension OrderList: AddsOrders {
 
  func addOrder(_ order: Order) {
 
      print("adding order")
 
      orders.append(order)
 
  }
 
}

Localization
 
Like colors, data sets, or icons, each target application must supply its
own localization files. In the JoesPizza app, add a new string catalog.
Manually add items as shown in Figure
 
 
Figure Default localizations
 

--- Page 823 ---
When the key can be used for the translation, then nothing specific
needs to be entered as the translation. It is a common practice to use
the default language text as the key and only fill in areas where it
should be something different.

Running the app
 
Inside the JoesPizzaApp file, two changes need to be made to use the
new libraries.
  
Add import PizzaApp at the top
 
Replace
  
With these two changes made, it is safe to delete the SwiftUI file,
ContentView and run the JoesPizzaApp scheme.
 
Click the add button at the top and add an order, optionally specifying
any meat and vegetable toppings. Any toppings added accidentally can
be deleted by swiping. Added orders will slowly change the progress
bar until it goes blank. The order card will change the background
once the time left is less than twenty-five percent. Orders can be
deleted by swiping.

countdown. In the AppUtility class, wrap the timeAsset code with #if

--- Page 827 ---
 
The other app
 
If an attempt were made to run the MamasPizza app, it would fail. The
assets have not been defined, nor has the ContentView() call been
swapped out with Remove the assets catalog from MamasPizza by
selecting it and pressing the delete key on the keyboard. Select Move
to Trash from the dialog. Copy the assets catalog from JoesPizza into
MamasPizza by holding down the option key and dragging the catalog
onto the MamasPizza group. Verify the copied file has MamasPizza as
the target membership. Do the same for the Localizable string catalog.
 
Change the font styles and toppings. When editing a file, such as
OrderView or what is shown in the preview reflects the currently
selected scheme in the toolbar. Using this technique, it is easy to verify
the view being worked on displays properly for the different target
apps. It should also be noted that the previews in the PizzaUI
framework will also reflect the colors of the target app.
 
Any new pizzerias can be easily added using the technique used to add
  
Create the new iOS app target.
 
Add the PizzaApp and PizzaUI libraries to the new app.

--- Page 828 ---
 
Copy the asset catalog and localizable string catalog.
 
Replace ContentView with OrderList in the SwiftUI App file.
 
Delete the SwiftUI ContentView file.
 
Customize the assets and localizations as needed.
OceanofPDF.com

--- Page 829 ---
 
Conclusion
 
Making a white label app is about providing a common functionality
with targeted areas where branding can be applied. Logos, fonts,
colors, and icons are common elements that are used to identify a
specific brand.
 
The example pizza app shows the technique of creating a white label
app, providing a separation of the primary app into a library, and
separating the app-defined user interface elements into another library.
This technique provides a separation of concern for easier
maintenance.
 
This book just scratches the surface of creating an iOS app. The
frameworks and techniques covered, while targeted specifically toward
iOS, can also be used for all of Apple’s platforms. What comes next is
up to you.
OceanofPDF.com
